801. MARY ANN WATERS was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of April, 1 shawl, value 8s.; 1 pelisse,value 5s.; 1 gown, value 5s., and 2 yards of lace, value 4s., the goods of William Cropper.
ELIZABETH CROPPER. I am wife of William Cropper; we live in Phoenix-street, Long-acre; the prisoner is my husband's cousin - she came to my room on the 3d of April, and staid there from twelve o'clock till twenty minutes before one - after she was gone I went to take my husband's dinner, and returned about a quarter before two o'clock - I found the door unlocked, and the prisoner's mother in my room - I examined and missed this property- I went to several pawnbrokers' shops, and found my property.
SARAH THOMPSON. I live in the same house with Cropper. On Monday last I was at the door, and saw the prisoner come in between one and two o'clock - she had nothing with her, but she came out with a blue bundle under her arm, and went up the court.
JOHN ANDREW SIMPSON. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Long-acre. All these things were pawned by the prisoner, on Monday, between one and two o'clock; I asked if they were her own - she said they were, and told me what they cost. I have known her three or four years.
Prisoner's Defence (written). The prosecutrix was in the habit of lending me things to pawn. I went on this night, and as she did not come in, finding her box open, I took them to pawn, meaning to return them on Saturday night, as usual.
ELIZABETH CROPPER re-examined. I once let her pawn two articles, but she did not, last Monday, say any thing about it, nor did I give her leave to take them.
JOHN ANDREW SIMPSON. She never pawned these things before, but has other things.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Confined Eight Days.

802. CHARLES WILSON and CHARLES DAVY were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 5 towels, value 10d.; 1 pinafore, value 4d., and an apron, value 4d., the goods of Mary Hutchinson, spinster,

[trialAccount t18260406-227]
803. RICHARD WEST and WILLIAM TOMS were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 3 iron cramps, value 20s., the goods of James Kerr; 3 saws, value 5s., and 1 vice, value 2s., the goods of John McAlister; and ELIZABETH TOMS and LOUISA FIELD were indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well-knowing them to have been stolen.
JAMES KERR. I am a chair-maker, and live in Clipstone-street, and have a workshop at No. 15 in that street. I missed three iron cramps on Monday, the 13th of February, about eight o'clock in the morning; the shop door had been broken open, and a piece of the lock broken off; there were three saws and a vice stolen belonging to my servant; I had seen them all safe on the Saturday night.
JOHN McALISTER. I am in Mr. Kerr's employ. I had been in the shop on Sunday, about five o'clock - I left it locked, and my tools were there; I went there next morning, about eight o'clock, and they were gone - a piece was knocked off the door, and a part of the lock was on the ground. Horne, the apprentice, was there before me.(Property produced and sworn to.)
SARAH LIMBRICK. I am a widow. I know William Toms and Elizabeth Toms - she is his mother; I work for Mr. Kerr; on the Friday or Saturday I went with a chair to William Toms, the younger; and asked him to carry it for me; he waited at the shop while I went in; and I gave him something to drink; I went to his lodgings on the Sunday evening, just at dusk; to borrow a pail; he lives close by me in Earl-street, Seven Dials; but a good way from Mr. Kerr's; I saw Louisa Field there, and Richard West came to borrow a cart; the next morning I went there again, and saw William Toms, who had sent for me; he said he had sent Louisa Field out with three saws to make 8s. for his rent, and he wanted some more money for some gentleman, who was to come at twelve o'clock; I saw, under the bed, three cramps and a vice, which I believe to be those produced; he asked me to go with his mother to pawn these cramps; we took one to Mr. Simpson; the next was pawned at the same place for 7s.; I went back and gave him the money; he then asked me to take the other; I said I did not like to take it; his mother said she would go with me; I took the cramp, and she took the vice; I pawned the cramp with Maynard, and went back and took him the money; his mother brought the money for the vice, and said she could not pawn it, but she had sold it for 5s. 6d.; the duplicates were put on the table; and he gave me and Toms 1s. a piece - he gave me the tickets to do what I could with them; I sold them for 4s. to a young man; I received information and told William Toms, next morning, that the duplicates were stopped; he said they had broken open the place with a crow bar and got the articles from Mr. Kerr's, but did not say whether he did it himself; he sent Louisa Field to the pawnbroker's to claim the tickets, and told her to say they belonged to her husband; she said she would say she got them of a man she met in the street, who gave them to her to let him sleep with her.
Cross-examined by Mr. FISH. Q. West was not present at either of these times? A. No.
WILLIAM TOMS. Q. When did you give me a chair to carry home? A. It was on the Friday or Saturday; I would not carry it myself; I hired your cart to remove my goods on the 11th of February - you had come to move my goods before the chair was taken home - I live at No. 11, East-street - I asked how he got the things out, and he told me with a crow-bar - the place is in a mews - he took the chair to the door, and I took it in of him - when a person was there they could see what was in the shop.
WILLIAM COUSINS. I apprehended Elizabeth Toms on the 17th of February, at the corner of Short's-gardens, St. Giles'; I took her to a house in Stanhope-street, where I had seen this vice, and the woman said she had been there a few days before to sell it; I then asked the prisoner how she could tell such a falsity when I took her, as to say she knew nothing about it - she said she did not like to acknowledge it.
BENJAMIN MORRIS. I am an officer. I took up West and Toms, at a public-house in Mitchell-street, St. Luke's.
JOHN ANDREW SIMPSON. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Long-acre. I have two saws pawned by Field, and the cramps pawned by Limbric, on the 13th of February; the officer came the next morning, he told me if any onecame to stop them - Louisa Field came in the afternoon, I detained her - the young man to whom Limbric had sold the tickets had called before, and I stopped the tickets.
CHARLES JAMES MAYNARD. I am a pawnbroker. I have a cramp pawned by Limbric.
THOMAS PACEY BURTS. I am a pawnbroker. I have a saw pawned on the 14th of February, by Field.
JANE MACKLIN. I keep a broker's shop in Stanhope-street, Clare-market. I bought this vice of Mr. Toms for 6s. 6d.
WILLIAM TOMS' Defence. Mrs. Limbric came to me to hire a cart to move her goods to the Seven-dials; I took the cart and met her husband - he said he could not find his wife, and told me to come next day - I then took the things to Earl-street, Seven-dials, and she said she would serve her master out, for he owed her 9s., and had only paid her 3s., and she would have two chairs from his shop, which she could get ten guineas for in the trade - that he had several chests of tools in his shop, and she was sorry she did not go the night before, and get his watch which she saw hung up.
LOUISA FIELD and ELIZABETH TOMS put in a written defence, stating that the witness Limbric had given them the property to pawn for her, and that she had been convicted of felony at Westminster Sessions.
SARAH LIMBRIC. I was drawn into it by that woman, who is one of bad character. I was three weeks in confinement about eight months ago, through having a handkerchief that Mr. Toms put in.
WILLIAM TOMS - GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
ELIZABETH TOMS - GUILTY. Aged 36.
Confined Three Months.
RICHARD WEST - NOT GUILTY.
LOUISA FIELD - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-228]
804. WILLIAM THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 2 half-crowns, 13 shillings, 3 sixpences, and 1 penny piece, the monies of Thomas Seares.
HANNAH BYRNE. I am servant at the Yorkshire Grey, public-house, in Eagle-street, which is kept by Thomas Seares. On the night of the 4th of April I was at home; Twinam ordered a pot of beer and change for a sovereign, to be sent next door to the chapel - I took the beer and 19s. 6d.; there were two half-crowns and some other silver - the prisoner stood at the door - he said, "The beer is to come here, have you got change?" I said,"Yes," and gave it to him - he gave me a farthing, and said, "Go back and get half a pint of rum, and take for it out of the sovereign;" I said it was not a sovereign, it was a farthing; he told me to go along, but I would not; I took hold of him and kept him till he was taken.
CHARLES TWINAM. I am near eleven years of age. On the night of the 4th of April, I was running up the street, and the prisoner said to me, "Go and order a pot of beer at the Yorkshire Grey, for Mr. Harris, next door to the chapel, and change for a sovereign," and when I came back he gave me a halfpenny, and said he would give me more when he saw me again.
JOHN WOODERSON. I saw the prisoner with Bird, at No. 1, Eagle-street - the prisoner was trying to get from her - it is a house of ill-fame - she said he had given her a farthing for a sovereign - the prisoner took some money out of his pocket, which fell on the ground - I picked up two half-crowns, several shillings, and four or five sixpences - I took him to the Yorkshire Grey.
WILLIAN GARROD. I am a constable. I took the prisoner, and found eight shillings and a half-crown on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I can only say I was on the spot and was taken - but I am not the person who took the money.
GUILTY. Aged 46.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-229]
805. WILLIAM STRAHAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of March, 1 sheet, value 9s., and 1 table cloth, value 10s., the goods of Mary White, widow; and 1 pair of drawers, value 2s.; 1 handkerchief, value 1s.; 1 nightcap, value 6d., and 1 pair of gloves, value 1s., the goods of George White.
MARY WHITE. I am a widow, and live in Ironmonger-street, St. Luke's. The prisoner lodged in my house for one month; I lost the articles stated, on the 9th of March, many of them belonged to my son George.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS CARPENTER. I am a pawnbroker. I have a table-cloth for which I gave this duplicate, but I do not know who pawned it.

Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-230]
806. ROBERT PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 2 shawls, value 1l. 15s., and 9 collars, value 9s., the goods of William Cozens.
WILLIAM COZENS. I am a linen-draper, and live at Kensington. On the 1st of April I saw the prisoner come up the steps and snatch these things off my counter - he run off, I pursued and took him - I found the collars on him - he was in a crowd and had disposed of the shawl.
Two witnesses to the prisoner's character were called, one of whom engaged to employ him.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Fined One Shilling, and Discharged
[trialAccount t18260406-231]
807. CHARLES REYNOLDS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 watch, value 30s., and 1 seal, value 1s., the goods of William Gregory.
WILLIAM GREGORY. I live in Europa-place, St. Luke's. The prisoner was in the habit of coming to see my brother-in-law; he came on the morning of the 31st of March to breakfast; he said he was in distress, and had been in the watch-house all night - I let him lie down; he got up between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, and I laid down - he then asked my wife to let him stop all night, which she did; the next morning my daughter brought down my watch, and put it on the table - the prisoner said it was a cold morning, and asked her to go and fetch a shirt collar which he had left up stairs - he then took the watch and went away; I had seen it safe about six o'clock that morning.
MARY GREGORY. I am Gregory's daughter. On the morning of the 1st of April the prisoner took up the watch, and desired me to go and fetch his collar - when I got up three or four stairs I saw him run out.
WILLIAM ANDERTON. I am a pawnbroker. This watch was pawned by the prisoner on the 1st of April, early in the morning; he came about eleven o'clock, with another man, and desired to see it - he recommended it to the other man to buy of him.
The prisoner pleaded distress. GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-232]
808. FRANCIS RONE was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 set of fire-irons, value 5s., the goods of Cornelius Norris.
CORNELIUS NORRIS. I live in Leather-lane. I had several sets of fire-irons in my shop - I lost this set on the 4th of April.
JAMES BURNHAM. I was in Beacham-street; on the 4th of April I saw the prisoner go to Mr. Norris' door and take the fire-irons - he ran about twenty yards, and then dropped them - I tried to stop him but could not, as I had a basket and a crow-bar in my hand.
ANN NORRIS. I saw the prisoner take the fire-irons; I am sure he is the man - he threw them down and Mr. Burnham took them up.
JOHN HINSLEY. I stopped the prisoner - there was no one before but a good many behind him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming down and heard the cry of Stop thief! I saw the fire-irons drop and I ran on.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-233]
809. SAMUEL RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 2 saws, value 16s.; 1 stock, value 15s.; 1 plane, value 4s., and 1 square, value 2s., the goods of Charles Meadwell; 1 case of drawing instruments, value 2l.; 1 book, value 2l.; 24 pencils, value 4s., and 1 key, value 1s., the goods of William Harper; 1 sash fillister, value 20s.; 1 stock and 36 bits, value 30s.; 3 planes, value 14s.; 1 carpenter's plough, value 13s.; 1 iron, value 6d.; 3 saws, value 18s.; 1 oil stone, value 1s.; 4 screw drivers, value 5s.; 16 chissels, value 10s.; 1 set of guages, value 4s.; 2 squares, value 6s.; 1 bevel, value 6d.; 1 pair of dividers, value 2s.; 2 hammers, value 3s.; 1 sash plane and templets, value 3s.; 3 guages, value 4s., and 1 basket, value 1s., the goods of John Stone; 1 carpenter's plough, value 16s.; 4 saws, value 20s.; 2 squares, value 4s.; 1 plane, value 3s., and 6 chissels, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Fletcher; 1 saw, value 6s.; 2 planes, value 5s.; 1 square, value 2s.; 1 pair of compasses, value 1s.; 1 pair of pincers, value 1s.; 4 chissels, value 2s.; 1 axe, value 2s.; 1 oil-stone, value 3s.; 1 box, value 1s.; 3 guages, value 6d., and 1 basket, value 3s., the goods Tempest Fletcher; 1 carpenter's plough, value 18s.; 17 chissels, value 15s.; 9 guages, value 6s.; 6 gimblets, value 2s.; 4 brad-awls, value 1s.; 13 pincers, value 2s.; 2 screw-drivers, value 3s.; 1 plane, value 3s.; 1 rule, value 2s.; 1 mallet, value 1s.; 1 square, value 1s.; 1 oil-stone, value 2s.; 1 hammer, value 1s.; 1 saw, value 5s., and 1 basket, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Stoner, and 1 plane, value 3s.; 1 square, value 4s.; 1 screw driver, value 1s.; 4 chissels, value 4s.; 1 fore-pin, value 1s.; 2 guages, value 1s., and 2 brad-awls, value 2d., the goods of Edward Stoner.
MR. ANDREWS conducted the prosecution.
ELIZABETH TURNER. I am servant to Mr. Soane, of Lincoln's-inn-fields. On the 20th of February, about nine o'clock in the evening, I saw a man in black,(not the prisoner,) on the top of the ladder of a building next door - he went over the wall; I asked what business he had there - he made no answer; I went round and saw two men talking - I called my fellow-servant, and saw the prisoner go up the ladder with the basket of tools on his back; I asked him what he wanted - he said he came for his tools; I said "If they are your tools why did not you take them before the place was locked;" he said "They were his tools, and he should take them when he liked;" my fellow-servant asked him if he worked there - he said he did, which I knew to be false, as the key of the building was hanging up in our kitchen - the other person had then got away - my fellow-servant, Catherine Wirlow, took the tools from the prisoner.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me get over the wall? A. No, going up the ladder with the basket at your back - you said that Mr. Payne had given you the key at eight o'clock that night to fetch the tools - you did not say a person gave them you to carry.
CATHERINE WIRLOW. I saw the prisoner standing with the basket of tools - I asked what he wanted there - he said it was nothing to me, that he was a carpenter and had come for his tools - I took the basket - I saw another person who looked at the prisoner and laughed; I pushed the prisoner into the yard and told him I would ask Mr. Payne in the morning if he sent him - the footman and the cook took the tools into the kitchen.
Prisoner. Q. You say you pushed me into the yard? A. Yes; you did not say there were two gentlemen who gave you the things to carry - a man came up and asked you what you knew of Mr. Payne; and you said you worked with him.
WILLIAM HENRICKSON. I was passing by - I asked the prisoner what he wanted - he said he was a carpenter, and two men had employed him to carry the tools; I asked him to go to the men - he went on to the corner of Carey-street, and there I saw two watchmen, and gave charge of him - he had attempted to run away.
CHARLES MEADWELL. I am a carpenter. I had left my tools, at No. 15, Lincoln's-inn fields - the men left work at half-past five o'clock, and no man had any right there afterwards - the key was hung up in Mr. Soane's kitchen - I had left the tools in my chest - they are worth more than a guinea.
WILLIAM HARPER. I am foreman of the works. I had locked the house, and given the key to the servant.
TEMPEST FLETCHER. Those articles are mine - I had left them on a bench in the building.
JOHN STONE. I claim this fillister; I had been at work with it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went to a sale at the next house, and cleared some of the things - a gentleman called on me and told me to go in the evening, and he would give me a job - I went about half-past seven o'clock and he called me to go up the ladder, which I did - he struck a light, and told me to take some baskets of tools into Carey-street to a public-house.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-234]
Middlesex Cases - Third Jury.
Before Mr. Serjeant Arabin.
810. WILLIAM HARRIS and JAMES AYRES were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 20lbs. weight of lead, value 5s., the goods of the Duke of Northumberland , and fixed to a building of his.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS NEALE. I am bailiff to the Duke of Northumberland. I have the care of his out-house, from which this lead was taken - it is in the parish of Isleworth.
JAMES GARDNER. On the 15th of March I was in what is called the Whale field, catching birds with Nathan Adams and Alexander Allen; we had been very quiet, and lying down; we got up to see if there were any birds, and saw the prisoner Harris, and a man named William Rowley, hiding something under a sand heap; after they were gone we went and raked the sand up, and found a sack with lead in it - we took and put it into a sand house, not far from the heap - we staid a short time longer, and then took up the nets - this was in the afternoon, but before dinner we went home, and returned in about half an hour to go bird's nesting, and saw the two prisoners sitting in the field with Rowley and White - they were swearing; I asked what was the matter, and Harris said some one had sprung the plant - I asked what he meant- he said some one had taken some lead of theirs - I asked what he would give if he knew who had it - he said"Two pots of beer and a punch of the head;" I did not stay any longer - next day I and a young man, named Hunt, were out bird's nesting - I told him what happened, and he said "We may as well go and get it" - we did so, and, as we were going down the town, we met Carver, the beadle, who stopped us - he took the lead - I went before the Magistrate and gave evidence.
NATHAN ADAMS. I was in the field with Gardner - I have heard what he has said, and it is true - I saw Harris and Rowley come to the spot; I saw Gardner and Hunt take the lead, but I was not with them when they were stopped - Ayres was not there the first time, but he was the second.
ALEXANDER ALLEN. I was with Gardner. I saw Rowley and Harris come into the field, and Earl stood at the gate - they put something into the sand - we went and took it out - it was then between twelve and one o'clock- I did not see the man come the second time.
THOMAS EARL. I was taken up about this matter, and examined by the Justice - he told me to say nothing, as it might hurt myself. I was at the Angel public-house, at Isleworth, on Friday evening, about nine o'clock- Harris, Ayres, and White were there; we all said, "Let us get a few turnips;" we walked by the Chaise and Horses public-house, and Harris said, "Let us go and get something worth while;" he took us up to Sion-lane - I did not know what he was going after till we came past Mr. Iron's; Ayres, Harris, and White then got over the pales - I could hardly get over, but Ayres eased me over, and we went on till we came to the cow yard - there are some pales round it, and some persons live in the little house by the side of the barn. Ayres and Harris got over the pales - White lifted me over; I waited there - Harris and Ayres got on the top of the cow shed; I heard them pulling the tiles off, and I ran to the other side of the pales. Harris called to White to ease it off the tiles - I did not then know what it was; I went up, and saw White under the tiles, with the lead on his shoulder. - Ayres and Harris got off the tiles, and carried it through the field, till they came to the hedge - Harris then lifted it on White's back, and they got on the sand heap in the Whale field, where they divided it into three pieces - this is one of them; Ayres sold one piece for 2s. 2d., and I sold one for 2s. 3d., and gave the money to Ayres, who shared it; the third piece was left in the sand house, under the straw; we went away; the next day Ayres, Harris, Rowley, and I, went birds' nesting; Harris and Rowley were a-head of us, and got into the Whale field, about a quarter of an hour before we did. I did not see the witness in the field; I heard Harris say somebody had sprang the plant, and I saw Gardner go and say, "What would you give to know who it was?" he said, "I would not mind two pots of beer and a punch of the head."
JOHN COE. I am a carpenter. I have compared this lead with that on the building of His Grace the Duke of Northumberland, and I have no doubt it had been taken from there - this is about a quarter of it.
JOHN CARVER. I am a beadle. I took the lead, and saw it fitted on the premises - it seemed to fit exactly.
COURT to THOMAS NEALE. Q. How lately had you seen the building before the lead was taken? A. Perhaps a fortnight or three weeks. I saw the lead fitted upon the building - the part that remains is fixed down.
HARRIS - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
AYRES - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-235]
811. CHARLES POWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of April, at Hillingdon, 1 duck, price 6s., the property of John Sibley.
JOHN SIBLEY. I live at Hillingdon, and am a labourer. - I keep ducks. On the 3d or April I was out - I had left my ducks safe.
WILLIAM NEIGHBOUR. I live near Sibley, at Hillingdon. Between five and six o'clock on the evening of the 3d of April, I saw two ducks on the river - one was brown and the other white - they were not a quarter of a mile from Sibley's. I saw the prisoner come within about three hundred yards of me, and shoot the brown duck; he waded up to his knees in the water, and got it, put it into his pocket, and went away. I went and told Mr. Sibley, and the prisoner was taken next day - I am certain he is the man there was another man with him, but the prisoner carried the gun, concealed under his coat.
ELIZA SIBLEY. On the 3d of April we had five ducks and a drake - one duck was a brown one - I missed that on this day. The river is across the field, about one hundred yards from the house; I have never seen the duck since. The prisoner lived at Uxbridge, and was taken the next day.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-236]
812. WILLIAM PURDY, HENRY DOWTY, and JOHN GRADY, were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 1 cask, value 3s., and 8 gallons of gin, value 3l., the goods of John Gyllyat Booth, and Felix Booth.
JOHN PARKER. I am clerk to Messrs. John Gyllyat,Booth and Felix Booth, they are rectifiers, and live at No. 55, Cow Cross-street, Smithfield. On the 18th of March, about a quarter-past nine o'clock, I went with the waggon to Mr. Weller's, in Old-street - the carman, Charles Merritt, was with me - the waggon contained two pipes, one puncheon, one hogshead, and the cask, which was taken out, which contains eight gallons and three pints of gin, while we were racking off the two pipes for Mr. Weller, about a quarter before ten o'clock, I saw Purdy standing loitering about the waggon; he put his hand through the side of the waggon to assist me in pushing off the pipe, and his other hand on the horse-cloths; we finished the two pipes in Mr. Weller's, and made the waggon secure, as we thought, and went into the house to put on our clothes; when we had been in about five minutes, Buckle gave me information - I went out and ran in the direction he told me, and saw Dowty, whom I had not seen about the waggon, with the cask on his shoulder; I collared him with my left hand and took the cask with my right - I asked him where he was going to carry it; he threw it down, with an intention of throwing it on my feet; he said he was a porter and took casks or any thing for any one who asked him; Purdy was then on the opposite side of the way; and as soon as Dowty threw the cask down, Purdy ran away; I called Stop thief! the carman pursued him; I took Dowty to the watch-house, and Purdy was brought there in about two minutes - I am certain he is the man I saw about; I know nothing about Grady.
CHARLES MERRITT. I am carman to Messrs. Booth. I saw Purdy waiting round the waggon all the time we were racking the gin; when we went in to put on our clothes some man came and gave an alarm; we ran out - I pursued Purdy, and saw Dowty carrying a cask of gin, which had been in the waggon; my partner came up to him and he threw it slap down almost on his goes - I pursued Purdy, because he ran way, and I thought he was as bad as the other - I knew him to be the man who had been waiting about the waggon.
JOHN BUCKLE. I am a broker. I live near Mr. Weller. On the 18th of March I saw a waggon at his door, delivering spirits - I saw the three prisoners and another man with them - they had been waiting about at a distance, while the spirits were delivering; one of them was at the window of a cook's shop, and the other at the corner of Ironmonger-row; when the men were gone in to get their clothes, the fourth man, who is not here, nodded to the other three, and Grady got on the waggon-wheel and handed out the cask (which seemed to me to be a barrel of gin) to Dowty, who was waiting for it; Purdy stood at the door all the time - the other was still at the cook's-shop window, at the corner of New-street - Dowty went along Old-street, and up Ironmonger-row - I went and told the carmen of it - Dowty and Purdy were soon after brought to the watch-house.
Cross-examined by Mr. BRODRICK. Q. Where were you? A. At my own door, nearly opposite Mr. Weller's, fifty or sixty yards off; I live with my father, who is a broker - I saw the carmen go into the house to put on their clothes; I had been looking about for half-an hour - I was afraid to call out for fear they should knock me down; I did not make any alarm till the cask was gone.
COURT. Q. Are you quite sure Grady is the man? A. Yes.
JOHN PRIMETT. I am a carpenter. I was coming through Old-street turnpike, and saw Purdy coming towards Golden-lane - I stopped him and gave him to the officer.
RICHARD VALENTINE HANDLEY. I am a yeoman. On the 18th of March, about eleven o'clock, I was coming up Old-street, and I saw a waggon, belonging to Messrs. Booth, standing at the prosecutor's door - I was stopping to tie my shoe, and saw Grady lifting a cask from the waggon; he put it on the hind-wheel - another person came up, put it on his knot, and walked off with it - it struck me something was wrong, and I watched him; he went up Ironmonger-row; I heard the cry that they had been robbing a waggon; Parker ran up Old-street, and I up Ironmonger-row; Parker seized the man; and I saw him stretch himself up and throw the cask down, as if to throw it on his legs; I have not the least doubt it was Grady who handed the cask out, but I will not swear to him.
Cross-examined. Q. You had but a short time to see him? - A. No; I was examined before the Magistrate, and my belief was the same as it is now.
JOHN TWEEDY. I am a constable. I was coming out of the watch-house when this happened; I saw some people running, and I put my prisoner back again; I saw Purdy run, pursued by another man; I pursued, and he was taken; I put him into the watch-house.
ROBERT LOCK. I am an officer. I took Grady on Saturday evening, the 18th of March, about eight o'clock.
PURDY'S Defence. I happened to stop at the corner of a street, and heard the cry of Stop thief! I ran like other people, but never saw either of the prisoners before.
DOWTY's Defence. I was going down Old-street; a man asked me to carry a cask, and said he would pay me for it; I went to the waggon and took it; he told me to go on.
GRADY's Defence. The man has sworn falsely; he swore to me by having a blue coat and leather gaiters; when I was taken I had a blue coat on, but no gaiters.
COURT to BUCKLE. Q. What coloured coat had Grady? A. A blue coat and a pair of leather gaiters above the knee, and he had them on when he was in the watch-house.
ROBERT LOCK. I do not think he had gaiters on when I took him; but I cannot swear he had not.
PURDY - GUILTY. Aged 19.
DOWTY - GUILTY. Aged 20.
GRADY - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-237]
813. CHARLES HUTTON, JAMES HANCOCK, and JOHN TAYLOR, were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of March, 200lbs. weight of lead, value 3l., the goods of Margaret Canham, fixed to a certain building.
JOHN HEZEKIAH ESSEX. I am a farmer, and live at Action. Margaret Canham is a widow and lives at Twickenham - she has a lodge at the entrance of her premises - I saw the lead secure on the top of it about a fortnight previous to its being taken - I saw the same lead before the Magistrate but was not present when it was fitted.
WILLIAM HALL. I am a constable. On the 10th of March, about half-past two o'clock, I saw Hutton going along Hounslow with a basket. Mrs. Hancock was close behind him - I followed him half a mile, and asked what he had got - he said some lead from Mr. Hancock's house, and he was going to Brentford to sell it - at the further end of Hounslow I got an officer, and we took Hutton - Mr. Emmerson and I went to Hancock, who is a shoemaker, and lives at the end of Hounslow - both the doors were fast - he went to the front door and I to the back; Mrs. Hancock said her husband was gone to Harlington, but a man said he had seen him just before - we then broke the door open and found him. I said to Hancock "You are my prisoner, you must go with me;" Emmerson went up stairs, and brought some lead, which Hancock said had been in Taylor's room; we asked where we could find Taylor; he said "If you go to Crawford-street, you will find him;" Emmerson went there and found him - we got a search-warrant a few days afterwards, and found some more lead in Taylor's box; as we were coming to London on the 11th, Hutton said "If we have taken the lead from Mrs. Canham's gate they can't swear to it now, because it is cut in pieces" - the other two prisoners were close to him, but made no observation. I believe they all lodged in Hancock's house.
JOHN EMMERSON. I am a Bow-street patrol. We apprehended Hutton with the lead - we then went to the house, and I found four pieces more, which Hancock said was Taylor's - I found two more pieces in Taylor's box - I took them to the office, and took two or three tradesmen to see where the lead was taken from; the lead here produced matched exactly to the top of the building; I knew that Taylor lived at Hancock's, but I did not know what room he occupied; I heard Taylor say he knew the lead came into the house the same night it was lost off the lodge - there were 87lbs. of it.
DANIEL BERKSHIRE. I am bailiff to Mrs. Canham. I saw this lead fitted on the building, and it matched exactly - I missed it on the 8th of March - I had seen it safe the day before.
HANCOCK's Defence. I let this man the room, and he had got the lead. I know nothing of him nor what was locked in his room.
HUTTON's Defence. Hancock asked if I had any thing to do - I said No; he asked me to carry it to Brentford for him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-238]
814. THOMAS PEARCE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 15 yards of flannel, value 10s., the goods of John Fowke.
JOB CLIFFORD. I am shopman to Mr. John Fowke, a draper, in Oxford-street. On the 14th of March, about three o'clock, a woman gave me information - I went out and found the prisoner about twenty yards from the door, with fifteen yards of flannel which had been standing within the door, surrounded by a chain.
THOMAS MORRIS. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I am very sorry - I was rather in liquor.
GUILTY. Aged 73.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Months.

815. JOHN MACQUIRE was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 2 pewter pots, value 2s., the goods of John Hinton.
JOHN HINTON. I keep the Hare and Hounds public-house, St. John's-wood . These pots are mine.
JAMES LAWS. I am a shoemaker. I saw the prisoner in St. John's-wood-lane, about eleven o'clock, with two quart pots. about 190 yards from Mr. Hinton's house - he put them down, took them up again, then walked round to the back of a house, and got in at the window - I heard him beating something - I went up to the place, and saw him beating one of the pots with a brick; I said"What are you beating these pots for?" - he said he was going to take the other pot home; he went out at the front of the house; I called to a person, and had him secured.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been for a pot of beer, and brought it down to the gardener - I went into the building, and put the pots down, but I did not beat them.
GUILTY. Aged 50.
Confined One Month.

816. THOMAS MALONEY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 pair of steps, value 5s., the goods of John Clough.
GEORGE WADDINGTON. I am a Bow-street patrol. Mr. Clough keeps an oil-shop, opposite Southampton-buildings. On the 27th of March, about seven o'clock in the evening I saw the prisoner looking into his shop; there was a pair of steps at the window to keep the people from the paint - they were open, he closed them, and put them under the window - he then went a little way, returned, and took them up, and put them down again - then took them up and went away - I secured him.
JOHN PARTIGER. I am porter to Mr. John Clough. I know these steps to be his.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming down Holborn, very much intoxicated, and a man gave me part of a pot of beer to carry these steps over to Southampton street.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Confined Two Months.

817. DAVID JENKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 2 half-handkerchiefs, value 2s., the goods of Edward Harris and Thomas Harris, the younger.
THOMAS ROBERTS. I am an officer. On the 24th of February, about a quarter before seven o'clock, I saw the prisoner, and two other persons, at Messrs. Harris' shop - I saw him take the two half-handkerchiefs from the inside of the shop door, and put them into his pocket - I followed him, and took him about three or four yards from the door - Bertraun, who was with me, found them in his pocket - his companions did not go with him.
ANGELIOUS BERTRAUN. I am an officer. I was in company with Roberts - I did not see the handkerchief taken, but I took the prisoner, and found the two half-handkerchiefs in his coat.
THOMAS HARRIS. I am in partnership with my fatherEdward Harris - we live in King-street, Soho, at the corner of Hayes'-court - I know these handkerchiefs to be ours.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.

818. ROBERT JOHNSON alias ROBERT LAW was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 gown, value 10s., the goods of John Holt.
JOHN HOLT. I keep the Duke's Head public-house, in Old Gravel-lane . On the 1st of April, about a quarter before four o'clock, the prisoner and another came in - I served them with a pint of beer - they drank it, and gave me half-a-crown to give change; I sent my wife up stairs to get it, and in the mean time the prisoner went into the yard; my man had some suspicion, and missed the gown from the yard, where it hung just before - he followed the prisoner into the tap-room, and said, "He has taken something off the line in the yard" - I collared him, took off his hat, and found the gown in it - he said he was very sorry, and it was his first offence.
WILLIAM DIXON. I am Holt's servant. I was in the kitchen when the prisoner went into the yard; I missed the gown - I followed him into the tap-room, and it was found in his hat.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went backwards, and met a friend, who said, "Will you mind this gown for me."
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-243]
FIFTH DAY. TUESDAY, APRIL 11.
Middlesex Cases, Third Jury.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
819. THOMAS MASON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 shawl, value 1s., the goods of Ellen Mullins; and 1 jacket, value 2s., the goods of William Wilkinson.
WILLIAM HALLETT. I am a cow-keeper, and live in Tyson-street, Clerkenwell. About half past five o'clock on the morning of the 24th of March, I went into my cow yard, and saw the prisoner coming out of one shed into the other, with a bundle under his arm: he turned into a stall where a chaff-engine was - he laid down his bundle there, went into another stall, and laid himself under the horse's head; I sent a man for the watchman, and the prisoner went into another stall; I then seat another man for a watchman; he attempted to make his escape, and I tried to seize him with one hand, having my light in the other, and some measures; he got from me, and ran to an open fence; I threw down what I had, and seized him by one leg; he then took a knife out of his pocket, and cut my two hands. One of the witnesses then came to me, and he cut at him in the legs; I tried to get the knife from him, but could not; he then cut at my legs, but with the back of the knife. I seized him by the throat - Ellen Mullins came, and got the knife from him - he was then taken to the watch-house - these articles were found in the manger.
WILLIAM WILKINSON. I am in the service of Mr. Hallet. I saw the prisoner in a stall in his cow shed, on the 24th of March; I knew him very well. I went for the watchman. We found this jacket in the manger - he was quite sober.
ELLEN MULLINS. I go to Mr. Hallett's to get milk. - I had left this shawl there five minutes before five o'clock that morning.
THOMAS UNDERWOOD. I am a constable. These articles were brought to me by Wilkinson.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been among the hay carts, trying to get some victuals. I got tipsy, and went to lay down in the manger, the shed being open.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years. (See page 258).
[trialAccount t18260406-244]
802. JOHN HITCHCOCK, JASPER HITCHCOCK, and ISRAEL DUNNING were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 4 live same pigeons, price 20s., the property of Charles Wighton.
CHARLES WIGHTON. I live in North-street, Poplar. - I missed four pigeons about four o'clock on the morning of the 5th of April - I had seen them safe about seven o'clock the evening before; the pigeon' house is about thirty feet from my house, in my garden. I found it broken open, and four gone out of the seven, which I found at the watch-house.
JOSEPH CRUCE. I am a watchman. About half-past two o'clock in the morning of the 5th of April, the three prisoners passed me in East India-road, and a little before three I heard a woman crying for the watchman - I ran to North-street, from whence the sound proceeded, and she told me there were three men getting on the leads at the top of the door; I ran after them, but did not see them. About half-past three o'clock I took (with some assistance) the three prisoners, whom I knew to be the persons I had seen at first; I found four pigeous on Jasper Hitchcock.
JAMES PURRY. I am superintendant of the watch. - Cruce brought the prisoners to the watch-house; I found four pigeons on Jasper Hitchcock: I made inquiries, and found where they belonged to. I had seen the prisoners pass the watch-house just before three o'clock.
JOHN HITCHCOCK's Defence. I was going home to the Barking-road, over the iron bridge, where I work.
JASPER HITCHCOCK's Defence. I bought the pigeons on the Tuesday evening.
DUNNING's Defence. I was going to Blackwall-dock, to look for work. I never saw these young men before.
JOHN HITCHCOCK - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Confined Six Months.
JASPER HITCHCOCK - GUILTY. Aged 16.
DUNNING - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.

821. MARY LOCKET DREW was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of April, two gowns, value 4s.; 8 yards of silk, value 1l. 8s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of George Syredd.
MARY SYREDD. I am the wife of George Syredd - we live in Arlington-street, Clerkenwell - we removed there three weeks ago; the prisoner and her husband were there at that time. On the 3d of April I missed a gown out of the drawer, which I had put it into, on the 30th ofMarch, and the prisoner saw me put it in - the silk was taken out of the one pair back-room.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know them? A. I am sure it is my gown by the pattern - I have since heard that all the prisoner and her husband had, was seized for 18s. - I believe they were in great distress, but I did not know it then.
WILLIAM CLUNES. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Liquor-pond-street. This gown was pawned by the prisoner in the name of Ann Parker, on the 31st of March.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you equally certain of her person before the Magistrate? A. Yes; I said it was a short dark woman, but I had forgotten her features - but the moment I saw her I knew her.
GEORGE STOWELL. I am a pawnbroker. I have a gown which I took in pawn from the prisoner on the 3d of April.
WILLIAM BLACKBOURN. I am a pawnbroker. I have a handkerchief which I took in pledge from the prisoner the 30th of March.
Prisoner's Defence. I had gone nine days without meat, and three days without bread - I have an infant six months old.
Three witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.

823. WILLIAM BOWDLE was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of March, 1 coat, value 1l., the goods of John Ward.
JOHN WARD. Prisoner. He gave me the coat to pawn and gave me the ticket of another; he told me to get that, and to pawn this. Witness. There was a duplicate of another coat which he took away - upon my oath I did not give him the coat to pawn.
GEORGE KING. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Oxford-street. On the 9th of March the prisoner pawned this coat with me for 10s. - he then gave me a ticket of another coat, in the same name, John Ward - he took that one out and went away.
JOHN PEARMAN. I met with the prisoner in a public-house, near Hyde-park-corner, on the 18th of March - he asked if I was out of service, and said he had two tickets, which he would sell for 5s. - they were of coats, which he bought at Ramsgate.
GUILTY. Aged 25.

WALTER OWEN HILLS. I am a pawnbroker. This pair of breeches were pawned on the 1st of April, about nine o'clock in the evening, in the name of John Bennett, by the prisoner.
ELIZABETH JONES. I bought this hat of the prisoner on the 1st of April, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening.

825. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 1 book, value 3s., the goods of John Wilton and William Wilkins Wilton.
WILLIAM WILKINS WILTON. I am a bookseller, in partnership with my father, John Wilton, we live in High Holborn. We lost a book, called "Humphrey Clinker," between seven and eight o'clock in the evening of the 6th of March - I was in the parlour, and a witness came and brought the prisoner in we searched him but could not find it- we took a light and looked in the yard just by our door and found the book.
JESSE PHILLIPS. On the 6th of March I was passing, this shop and saw the prisoner and another young man - the prisoner took a book off a small shelf and put it into his trousers' pocket; I collared him and pushed him into the shop - we afterwards took a candle and I found the book about two yards from the place.
Prisoner. Q. You said there were three or four persons. A. No; you resisted, and turned to go up the gateway.
RICHARD GARDNER. I was called to take the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I was standing near the window, with three or four other persons - as soon as the alarm was given they all ran away, and I stood still.
JURY to JESSE PHILLIPS. Q. Was it possible for the prisoner to have thrown the book up the gateway from where he stood? A. Yes, it was; the prosecutor had not missed it; there was a gas-light in the window, buthe took it him from under the board; I took hold of him immediately, but he turned his back and gave himself a twist round.
Prisoner. Q. Were not you standing in the gateway? A. No; I was not; I did not state so at Bow-street; I went up there, but did not see him throw the book.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.

826. JOHN BARNETT was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 sovereign, the money of James Wright.
JAMES WRIGHT. I am a sailor, and live in Hall's-rents, St. Catherine's. I went to a shop in Ratcliff-highway, on the 28th of March; I saw the prisoner at the door, who asked me if I wanted to buy a suit of clothes or a jacket; I went in and bargained for one for 12s.; I put down a sovereign on the counter - he took it, put it towards his pocket, and kept it there a few minutes, when he threw down another, and said I had given him a counterfeit, and asked where I got it; I told him to put the jacket by, and I would call again when I got out - I looked at the sovereign, and it was not the one I gave him - it was whiter and lighter; I had taken two sovereigns of my Captain that day, and had changed one at Mr. Farmer's shop - they were both alike and of the same colour.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. How many shops had you been to? A. Only one; there was a young man with me, who is gone to sea; the prisoner came down the next morning after the officer had been for him; my Captain was before the Magistrate; there was a man and a woman in the shop; a man gave my shipmate the jacket, and he was putting it up.
WILLIAM WILSON. I am an officer. The prisoner is shopman to Mr. Hart; he is not the owner of the shop; I went down to the shop, and the prisoner came to the office immediately.
NOT GUILTY.

827. PATRICK JOSEPH CASHMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of March, 1 brass fender, value 1l. 15s., the goods of Joseph Adams.

828. JOHN COWLEY and THOMAS FOSTER were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 75lbs. weight of flour, value 13s., the goods of William Aldridge.
WILLIAM ALDRIDGE. I am a baker, and live in Coppice-row, Clerkenwell. The prisoner Foster was in my service; on the 28th of March two persons came from the watch-house, and asked if I had lost any flour - they had some in a bag; the bag was not mine, but the flour was; it had been in my flour-house which opens by a flap into the street; I missed 80lbs. of flour out of a sack which was full the night before.
Prisoner FOSTER. My master told me to put some fresh and some old flour into the bin; I had not enough to make my dough, and I took that out of the sack. Witness. No, there was enough without opening that at all; he wanted about 80lbs. to make his dough - there was enough of the other flour which I saw in the trough; I can tell within a peck; I saw the flour at the watch-house, and it was precisely the same as that in the sack.
JOSEPH ROOT. I am a watchman of Clerkenwell. I was going to get a light of the next watchman, and met Cowley coming up Pear-tree-court, about three hundred yards from Mr. Aldridge's, with a bag on his shoulder - I called twice to know what he had got, but he gave no answer - I went up and said, "What have you got here?" he put it down, and said he found it; I said, "You must go to the watch-house, and take the flour;" he said he would not - I called another watchman - we secured him.
Prisoner COWLEY. Q. Did I not say I had picked it up? A. No; you did not stop till I put my hand on it - you then pointed to a corner which I had passed two or three times just before, and said you had found it there.
THOMAS GARNER. I am the watch-house keeper. Cowley was brought there about one o'clock on the 28th of March, with a bag of flour, and when I went out I knocked at Mr. Aldridge's cellar, and asked if he was there; I was told by Foster, (to the best of my belief,) that he was not - I left word that some flour was found, and was at the watch-house, and if he came there he might see it; I think the answer I received was, "Very well;" after Mr. Aldridge was up I went to his house again - he came and saw the flour, which he said he believed was his - he then searched over his flour and missed it; he inquired what house Cowley belonged to, and he said the Marquis of Gransby, which was the same house his man belonged to.
COURT to WILLIAM ALDRIDGE. Q. Who did you leave in the cellar? A. Only Foster - he was to remain there, and call me up at three o'clock, which he did, but did not say any one had been inquiring for me; I have a sample from the sack, and it corresponds with this.
JAMES PERRIN. I am a baker. I have compared the flour that was found with that in the sack, and they appear to me to be both the same.
JOHN BOWYER. I received the sack of flour from the witness and gave it to Mr. Garner.
FOSTER's Defence. This man is quite a stranger to me; he does not belong to the house of call to which I do; Mr. Aldridge is not the only gentleman who has the same sort of flour.
COURT to WILLIAM ALDRIDGE. Q. Do you know any thing about Cowley? A. They both used one house, and I have heard that he and my man were about together; I do not think Foster would have wanted any flour to make up the dough, but if he did, it could not be near the quantity missing; I am certain there was nearly, if not quite, enough - I can tell within a peck.
COWLEY - GUILTY. Aged 43.
FOSTER. GUILTY. Aged 30.
Transported for Seven Years.

SARAH THOMAS. I am the wife of John Thomas, a green-grocer - we live in Ratcliff-highway. On Sunday, the 26th of March, there were three half-crowns in my till, and a great deal more silver; I left the shop about ten o'clock, for about a minute, and when I returned I saw the prisoner in the shop - I had seen him on the Sunday before - the till was open; I said, "I believe you are the little girl who robbed me last week;" I put my hand on her shoulder, and drew out the till, and said, "You little hussey, you have robbed me again;" she had her hand clenched, which I opened, and found in it two half-crowns; I had taken half a crown a few minutes before.
CHARLES TICKLE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and bring the money.

831. CHARLES CURRIE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 20 yards of stair carpet, value 35s., the goods of George Chowles.
GEORGE CHOWLES. I live in North Audley-street, and am an upholsterer. On the 1st of April, about eleven o'clock, I went to Bayswater, and while there I received information - I came to town, and missed these three pieces of carpet, which are mine.
JOHN LEWIS. I am a Bow-street patrol. Between twelve and one o'clock, on the 1st of April, I saw the prisoner in Brook-street, about half a mile from Mr. Chowles'; he had the carpeting concealed - when I came up to him he dropped it and ran - there was another boy with him, who ran another way.
RICHARD BOWDEN. I saw the officer throw down this carpet by my door.
Prisoner's Defence. I went down the street - a young man came up to me, and said he would give me sixpence to take it to Bond-street - he rested it on the post, and the officer came and asked what I had got - I said I did not know - he said, "Who does it belong to?" I said, "That young man;" he ran away, and the officer after him - I then thought he had not come by it honestly, and I ran off.
Mr. CROWLES re-examined. I had left three painters in the shop.
NOT GUILTY.

833. ELIZABETH NICHOLLS was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of February, 1 bed, value 1l. 15s.; 2 blankets, value 5s.; 1 pillow, value 2s., and 1 pillow case, value 6d., the goods of Charles Jones, in a lodging room.
JANE JONES. I am the wife of Charles Jones - we live in Little Union-street, St. George's, East. I let a furnished room to the prisoner at 5s. a week - she had not completed her week; I went out to work on the 2d of February, and came home about half-past eight o'clock at night - she was gone, and I missed this property; she never returned to pay the rent; I am quite sure of her person - I do not know how she gets her living.
Prisoner. She was perfectly aware that I pawned the things, and I gave her part of the money. Witness. I knew nothing about her pawning the things, and never gave her leave to do it; she had been in the house with a lodger of mine, for a few weeks before she lived with me.
JOHN FIELD. I am foreman to a pawnbroker, in High-street, Shadwell. I took in a bed of the prisoner, to the best of my recollection, on the 1st of February, on which there was 5s. more advanced the day after.(Bed produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I gave her part of the money towards the rent - another person took the room besides me; I had left the room, but the other person was there.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Transported for Seven Years.

834. ELEANOR RYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of March, 1 silver thimble, value 1s., and 1 earring value 1s., the goods of Amelia Mills, widow, her mistress.
AMELIA MILLS. I am a widow, and live in Titchfield-street. The prisoner was my servant - I had an officer to search her, and he found on her a thimble and an earring, which I had seen about a fortnight before in my kitchen-drawer - I had accused her of taking them - she said nothing.
WILLIAM REED. I am an officer. I searched the prisoner, and found a thimble and a gold ear-ring, which the prosecutor said were her's; the prisoner said she brought them with her.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I found the thimble on the floor - I put it into my pocket and forgot it - my mistress said she had lost the ear-ring for three years.
GUILTY. Aged 19.

[trialAccount t18260406-259]
835. THOMAS RAWLINS, JOHN PRITCHARD, and CATHERINE DONOVAN were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 watch, value 4l.; 2 seals, value 3l.; 1 ring, value 10s.; 1 watch chain, value 1s.; 1 waistcoat, value 10s.; 1 handkerchief, value 6d.; 1 pair of scissars, value 1s., and 1 knife, value 1s., the goods of Charles Tanner, from his person.
Mr. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
CHARLES TANNER. I am an independent gentleman. - On Easter Tuesday, the 28th of March, I spent the evening with some friends - I was rather forward in liquor, and met the prisoner Donovan in Oxford-street, near St. Giles's - she invited me home with her, and I went, not knowing where she was going to take me - I had not much judgment. She took me to Bainbridge-street, St. Giles's, as I afterwards found - we went into a back room up one pair of stairs, and it was agreed that I was to pass the night there. I undressed, and she took off her gown, but was very slow in getting her things off. I had two purses in my side pocket, containing silver and gold, and to the best of my recollection, I had a watch; I am certain I had had it two hours before. I cannot say but what it might have been extracted from my pocket before - I had come from the City, and had been walking about; I had a blue cotton handkerchief in my pocket. The prisoner then said I should give her the money before we went to bed, but I was quite unwilling to remain, from the appearance of the place, which was very mean and shocking - I said I would go away; she then said she would have some money, but I was unwilling to show where my purses were, as my clothes were off, and on a chair; I therefore declined giving her any at that time; she then sat down on a chair, took up the coat, and searched the hinder pockets; she threw the coat about several times, but did not discover the purses; she arose up again, and said if I would not give her some money she would soon call in some person who should make me - she then went outside the door, and instantly Pritchard came in; I had not heard her say anything outside the door. She came and stood on one side of me, quite close - he stood on the other - she began telling him that I would not give her any money - at that moment Rawlins came in at a window - they both stood by me, but said nothing. Donovan then took up the poker, held it over my head, and said she would dash my brains out, unless she had some money; she then struck me with her first under my right ear; I saw I had better concede to any thing then lose my life. I told her my property was in my waistcoat pocket; she then went back to the table, snatched up the waistcoat, and blew out the candle; they then all three went away with my waistcoat. I put on my clothes as fast as I could, went out, and called the watchman, Ryan - he went with me up to the room, and found nothing there. I described them to him - as we were going up the stairs I found my pocket handkerchief. We then went three or four houses off, up to the second floor - he knocked at the door, and said, "Let me in;" they said, "Who is there?" and I think it was the woman, who said, "We are all in bed;" the watchman said if they did not let him in he would burst the door open - the door was then opened; my waistcoat was laying on the table; the three prisoners were in bed together, and another woman, who is not here - Ryan and the other watchman made them all get up - Donovan was quite dressed, as when she left me; I believe the men had their coats off. I am certain the waistcoat was the one she took from the room.
Prisoner DONOVAN. Q. Did not you tell me I might take the waistcoat, and did I not take two letters and two small keys out of it, and say I would go and get a light? A. No.
TIMOTHY RYAN. I am a watchman. On the 28th of March I saw Donovan come into the street, between one and two o'clock - and in the course of ten minutes afterwards she came out with a candle, and asked me for a light, which I gave her; she then went back into Jones'-court. I was going down Bainbridge-street between two and three o'clock, and the prosecutor said, "I am robbed by a girl and two men;" I went with him to the room, and found nothing there - I told him to be silent, as I had seen the girl come out of No. 6, and he had come out of No. 10. I got the next watchman, and went to No. 6 - I found the handkerchief on the stairs; I went up, and told them to open the door; one said, No, the door was locked, and what did I want - I said they must open the door - some one did so; we entered, and the three prisoners and another woman were in bed together - Donovan was dressed, and the others had their clothes off, except their shifts and shirts. The prosecutor said, "I can swear that is the girl, and these are the men;" he had described them before. I found this waistcoat on the table.
MORRIS LEONARD. I was called to assist Ryan - he has stated what is correct.(Waistcoat and Handkerchief produced and sworn to.)
DONOVAN'S Defence. These two young men are innocent. I never saw them till I went up into their room.
RAWLINS' Defence. I was in bed about twelve o'clock- about three I heard a knock; Donovan came in, and asked for a light; I said there was no light and no fire; she had not been there many minutes before she heard footsteps on the stairs; she appeared very confused, and ran to the bed - the door was only on the latch.
RAWLINS - GUILTY. Aged 18.
PRITCHARD - GUILTY. Aged 16.
DONOVAN - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Of stealing, but not from the person.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-260]
836. ELEANOR NIHILL was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of March, 11 spoons, value 20s.; 2 gowns, value 4s.; 2 shawls, value 6s.; 1 pelisse, value 3s.; and 5 caps, value 3s., the goods of John Hovee, her master.
SARAH HOVEE. I am the wife of John Hovee, and live at the Repository, in Goswell-street; the prisoner had been with us a fortnight. On the 8th of March, I rang the bell, about half-past six o'clock, for her to get up - she did not answer, and I went up to her room and found she was gone - I came down and found the front door open - I missed all this property - I saw her in custody, about twelve o'clock that day.
WILLIAM DUFFIELD. I am stable-man to Mr. Hovee. I met the prisoner in Chiswell-street, about twelve o'clock- I told her her master and mistress were out after her - she offered me something in a handkerchief, which I would not take - but a young man, who was with me, took it home, and I detained her.
RICHARD FIELD. I was with Duffield. The prisoner said I might have the things if I would let her go - I took them, and left him in charge of her - there were some articles in the handkerchief.
JOHN WILLIAM HARRISON. I took the prisoner. Onthe 8th of March, about 12 o'clock; Mr. Hovee delivered four table-spoons and tea spoons to me - the prisoner had a gown, a cap, and a shawl on her person - and she told me if I would go to Long-alley, I should find some more - I did so, and found more in Sweet-apple-court, and the other tea-spoons at a pawnbroker's.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy.
Transported for Seven Years.

The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-262]
838. EDWARD CLARK SPENCER was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 pair of sheets, value 2s.; 1 shirt, value 4s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 6d.; 2 towels, value 6d.; 1 night cap, value 2d., and 1 apron, value 6d., the goods of George Kent, from the person of William Kent .
ELIZABETH KENT. I live by Stepney church. On the 13th of March, about twelve o'clock, I sent my grandson, William Kent, with a bundle, containing these articles, to take about two hundred yards, to his mother's, to be washed - he returned and told me they were gone.
WILLIAM KENT. I am eight years and a half old. My grandmother sent me with a bundle to my mother's for the things to be washed; the prisoner met me, and asked me to go down Globe-lane to get two bundles of silk; and said, he would hold my bundle - I gave it to him - and when I came back he was gone.
WILLIAM MOGRIDGE. I am an officer. I was walking down Mile-end-road, and saw the prisoner running across the field with a bundle - I pursued, and took him near Stepney church - he said he had met his own mother in Whitechapel-road, and she gave him the bundle.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence, (written). I was persuaded by Charles Gigg, a neighbour, to go to a bull-hunt - as we were in the road, he said "There is a boy with a bundle, we will frighten him" - he told me to send him for two skeins of silk and hold his bundle the while - and when I had it, Gigg ran away and told me to follow - I became alarmed, and asked him to take the bundle - but he said two men were following us.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18260406-263]
839. ELEANOR SHEA was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 bag, value 1d.; 1 snuff-box, value 6d.; 19 sovereigns, and 2 half-sovereigns, the property of William Hanington, from his person.
WILLIAM HANINGTON. I keep a public-house in George-street, Grovernor-square. I was at the Red Lion, public-house, Duke-street, Manchester-square, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening of the 12th of March - I was a good deal the worse for liquor, and happened to go in there, when I saw the prisoner, whom I had known for upwards of twenty years - she once kept a house near me; she was drinking with another person, named Ryan, who had a little girl with her - I spoke to the prisoner, and gave her something to drink; I went home with them to their room in Gee's-court, Oxford-street - I should think it was between ten and eleven o'clock, but I am not certain; we had some more to drink there; I believe the little girl went out for liquor, but I am not certain; I went to sleep on the floor, and about half-past four in the morning, I awoke; I went out into the passage to go home - I missed my bag, with nineteen sovereigns, and two half-sovereigns, and requested them to give it me; I did not miss my snuff-box till afterwards; I went to their house; I changed something out of it after I met them at the public-house - it was in my left-hand breeches pocket - when I asked them for it they both laughed at me; the watchman happened to be going by and I called him - he found the snuff-box in the room, and the officer found the bag on the Monday following.
CATHERINE RYAN. I am the daughter of Johanna Ryan - she lives in Gee's-court - the prisoner was an acquaintance of hers. I am in the workhouse - I was at the public-house, and saw the prosecutor come in, and have some gin and water; he did not speak to the prisoner, but she took hold of his glass and drank out of it - my mother and I went home, leaving them there; when we had been at home about an hour, they came there together - the prosecutor sent me out for half a pint of rum, which they drank; they then sent for half a gallon of beer; I went out for another half pint of rum, and afterwards I saw the prisoner pull up her clothes, and dance before the prosecutor; my mother said she ought to be ashamed of herself, and then she sat upon his knee for a quarter of an hour; we had not much candle - the prosecutor then got up to go away, but he fell down on the floor, and soon afterwards the candle went out - I then went to bed - when I awoke in the morning the prisoner and my mother were laughing at the prosecutor; I then went and sat on the stairs - the prosecutor called the watchman - he could not open the door, and the watchman broke in - he got another watchman, and they searched, but could not find the money - they went to the watch-house.
COURT. Q. Do you recollect any thing about a watch? A. Yes; the prisoner went up to the prosecutor, and pulout his watch - she said "By G-d he has got a good watch," and then put it in again - she then went to his other side, and took out a bag of sovereigns - he awoke, and said "What are you at?" he then pushed it into his pocket again, and she went and sat down on the bed - I then went to sleep, and did not see any more - the candle went out.
WILLIAM VARNDILE. I am a watchman. I was calling the hour of half-past three, and heard a great noise - I came on towards the passage, and heard a rustling - I said "Who is there?" and this gentleman said "It is I;" I said "Open the door;" he said "I can't;" I then told him where the bolt was, and we got it open; I went in and took the prisoner - I found a half-sovereign, four shillings, four sixpences, and four-pence halfpenny, on her; the half-sovereign she took from some part of her clothes, on her right breast - she had before said she had no money about her.
THOMAS HENDERSON. I am a watchman. I searched Ryan, and found on her a half-sovereign, six shillings, andfive-pence halfpenny, the prosecutor's snuff-box, and the key of his watch.
THOMAS PERRING. I was serjeant of the night. I was standing in Oxford-street, when the watchman came to me, and said a person was robbed at No. 6, Gee's-court; the prosecutor said he had lost upwards of twenty sovereigns - the prisoner said she had a shilling or two, and a few halfpence - she was then searched, and I saw her take something from her right breast, which I could not get from her hand.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I say I took that from my duplicates? Witness. No; you was in liquor. I then went back to the room, and on turning the ashes up we pulled out the bag, with nineteen sovereigns and two half-sovereigns in it.
ROBERT WILLIAMS. I am an officer. I went with Henderson, and found this bag, with nineteen sovereigns and two half-sovereigns in it.
WILLIAM HANINGTON. This is my bag - here is a piece of the same cotton it is made of.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating, that she had met Ryan at the public-house, and, being intoxicated, stopped at her house all night, and was asleep when the robbery was committed.
GUILTY. Aged 50.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-264]
840. LOUISA PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of February, 3 sovereigns, the money of William Pelpford, from his person.
WILLIAM PELPFORD. I am an excavator. I fell in with the prisoner in the City-road between twelve and one o'clock on the 26th of February; I had been drinking pretty freely, and had about nine sovereigns; I went home with her to a house in Wellington-place; I asked what I was to give her; she said 1s. 6d.; I took a sovereign out, and laid it on the table, and said "I will have my money taken care of before I have any thing to do with you;" I then pulled out two sovereigns, she snatched them out of my hand, and ran down stairs; I ran after her, but was stopped in the passage by a girl or two, and a young man; she escaped, but was taken the next night; I am certain she is the same girl - she was a stranger.
FRANCIS LEONARD. I am a watchman. I heard a great disturbance on my beat, and a call for Watchman! the prisoner ran by me; I did not stop her because I thought there was something serious up the court; I then met the prosecutor, who said he had been robbed; I told him I knew her, and should see her again.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the man; he would go home with me, and followed me to the door, and came in; I said"What do you want here;" he said "Do you think I have no money" - he took out three sovereigns and threw them down on the table; I said were they for me - he said Yes; after being in the room some time, because I would not give them to him, he called the watchman.
NOT GUILTY.

841. DENNIS KELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of Thomas Russell Drury, from his person.
THOMAS RUSSELL DRURY. I am a printer, and live in the Strand. On the 24th of February, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, I was in Long-acre, walking with my sister; I felt my handkerchief taken from my pocket, and turned round immediately; I saw the prisoner attempting to pass me with it in his hand; I collared him, and he threw it down by the door of a wine vaults - my sister picked it up.
ISAAC SAYER. I am an officer. I have the handkerchief, and took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to).
GUILTY. Aged 15.

[trialAccount t18260406-266]
842. GILES SUSSEX was indicted for embezzlement.
JAMES GARRETT. I a toy manufacturer, and live at Hoxton, in the parish of St. Leonard's, Shoreditch. The prisoner had been in my service fourteen or fifteen months - he carried out goods and received money, which he was to return - he was paid weekly; I sent him on the 14th of February to Mr. Clementson with some goods, and to receive the money; David Evans went with him; I did not see him again till I saw him at Worship-street Office; he had given me no notice of leaving; he should have accounted to me for the money as soon as he returned; he had a basket and truck with him.
WILLIAM CLEMENTSON. I am a newsman and dealer in toys. I deal with the last witness; the prisoner brought some toys to me on the 14th of February; I paid him ten sovereigns and a ten pound note; Evans and him were together; I have the number of the note which I gave him.
DAVID EVANS. I went with the prisoner to Mr. Clementson's; I saw him pay the prisoner in ten sovereigns and a 10l. note - after we had delivered the goods we went to have some refreshment - he left me in a public-house, and said he would go and get a bit of good old Cheshire cheese, and I saw him no more.
ROBERT TURLE. I keep the Three Tuns public-house in Bridgewater-square. The prisoner came there on the 14th of February, and had some liquor - he returned again in the evening with a woman, and they staid till I was about to shut up the house; I was given to understand he had some money about him, and said if he liked to leave it with me he would know where to find it; he left me a 10l. note, and called again two days afterwards and had two sovereigns out of it, and ordered a leg of mutton to be cooked; I gave Vann 5l. 15s. 6d., which was the balance - the note was paid to a butcher, and then to a person in Smithfield.
THOMAS FAIRCHILD. I gave ten sovereigns for the note, and paid it away.
THOMAS VANN. I received information from the prosecutor, and found the prisoner at the public-house - Mr. Turle said he had left the note with him.
Prisoner's Defence. When I drink a drop I do not know what I do.
GUILTY. Aged 63.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18260406-267]
843. JOHN WHEATLEY was indicted for bigamy.
HENRY FELSTED. I am a vellum-binder. I work for Mr. Thomas, in Cornhill - my sister Elizabeth married the prisoner in 1809, at St. Olave, Southwark - I was present and saw them married; I signed the register - he was then a shoemaker - my sister had been in service and lefther place to marry him; he lived with her about six months, when she was so ill-treated that she left him and went to service, because he would not support her; she died at the end of last July.
Cross-examined by Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not your sister change her name? A. Yes; while she was in service she took the name of Groves: Mr. Groves was a tailor- he is dead I believe; they lived together as man and wife about seven years.
ELIZABETH ANN ROGERS. The prisoner married me on the 20th of October, 1823, at St. Matthew's, Bethnalgreen. I had been in service, and left it to marry him - I had known him from the July before; I had no fortune; he behaved very far from well to me - I was never happy - we had no children; no one told me to bring forward this case, but I heard he had another wife.
Cross-examined. Q. Is your mother here? A. Yes; I recollect the prisoner going to the police-office with my mother - it was to inquire whether his wife was dead; he said he had not seen her for nine years; I had no suspicion that he was married when I married him.
JAMES LEA. I am an officer, of Lambeth-street. I produce the certificates of the marriages and a burial.
These certificates were here read, the first was of a marriage between John Wheatley and Elizabeth Felsted, on the 17th of May, 1809, at St. Olave, Southwark - and the second of John Wheatley and Ann Rogers, on the 20th of October, 1823; also a certificate of the burial of Elizabeth Groves, at St. Mary-le-bone, on the 1st of August, 1825.
- ROGERS. I went with the prisoner to Worship-street, before he married my daughter Ann; he came to my house as a single man - but he was accused of being married - he said he had been, but his wife was dead, he believed, and he would go and inquire whether he might marry again; there was no Magistrate there, and the clerk would not decide the question, but said he might advertise his wife.
GUILTY. Aged 35.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-268]
844. JAMES BLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 1 saw, value 5s. the goods of Nathaniel Hudson.
NATHANIAL HUDSON. I am a carpenter. On the 16th of February I left my saw in Black-horse-fields, on the carcass of a house, at twelve o'clock, and missed it at one.
JOSPEH HARROW. I am a bricklayer. About ten minutes past twelve o'clock I was on the top of a building, and saw the prisoner coming along with the saw wrapped up in a dirty cloth, about a quarter of a mile from where it was taken - I called to him, and asked him how he got it - he said he had found it; I said, "If you will go back, and can show the place, you shall be let go;" he went back, put it against a heap of bricks, and said he had found it there.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I met a boy who asked me to take care of this saw for him.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Seven Years.

845. JOHN BARRATT was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of March, 60lbs. weight of lead, value 20s., the goods of John Smith and William Hill Stamp, and fixed to a certain building.
ROBERT BOYD. I am a builder. I know where this lead was taken from - I afterwards saw it fitted - it tallied exactly; I have no doubt it came from there - the building belongs to John Smith and William Hill Stamp; on the 22d of March, about a quarter-past eight o'clock, I saw the prisoner and another person drawing a sack from the area of this carcass, and when they got it to the wall joining the side of the bank, I saw another youth heaving it on the prisoner's back; I pursued, and met him with the lead - the other escaped; the lead was gone from off the gutters of four houses - there were seven or eight hundred weight in all.
HUGH CRAIG. I am an upholsterer. I saw the prisoner draw the bag from the area - I was not aware what was in it; I called Boyd, who followed him; I turned the bag out, which contained the lead.
ROBERT TEASDALE. I am watch-house keeper. The prisoner and the lead were brought to me; I saw it fitted on the building, and it matched exactly.
JOHN SMITH. I am part owner of this property, in trust for Mr. Joseph Biddulph.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.
