876. ABRAHAM BATES and WILLIAM BRIGGS were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 3s. the goods of David Richardson, from his person.
DAVID RICHARDSON. I was in Leadenhall-street on the 8th of March, and a person told me my pocket was picked; I missed this handkerchief, which I had safe two minutes before.
ROBERT HESKETH. I was in Leadenhall-street on the 8th of March, and saw the two prisoners near the prosecutor - Briggs drew the handkerchief from his pocket, and handed it to the other; it was then thrown on the ground - I secured them, and called to the prosecutor. I found a watch on Bates.
BATES' Defence. I saw this young man passing a cart, and the officer came and said I had taken the handkerchief.
BRIGGS' Defence. I had not had the handkerchief.
BATES - GUILTY. Aged 16.
BRIGGS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-304]
877. WILLIAM ADAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 65 pieces of printed paper, value 1s. 2d.; 296 wood-cuts, value 4s. 8d.; 150 copper-plate impressions, value 5s.; 70 printed books, value 11s. 8d.; 3 printed books, value 3s.; 13 books, value 2s., and 86 other printed books, value 16s. 8d., the goods of John Cumberland, his master.
SAMUEL TURNER. I am a constable. Mr. Cumberland desired me, on the 18th of March, to go to his warehouse in Blackfriars; the prisoner came out, and I stopped him - I took him to a yard opposite, and searched him - I found these covers in his hat, and these wood-cuts. I took him to the Compter, and them went with Mr. Cumberland to his lodging, where we found some copper-plates, cuts, and other things. When I took them out of his hat I asked what they were - he said waste paper, and he wanted to paper his room with them - I asked if his master allowed him waste paper, and he said No.
TERENCE TIERNEY. I am a bookseller, and live at the corner of Rossell-court, Drury-lane. Some of Dolby's Plays were brought to me by a female, whom I have seen since at Guildhall - she told me her name was Shields.
WILLIAM WELKER. I am a bookseller. Between the 10th and 17th of March, a woman brought some books to my house - I saw her at Guildhall; she said there that she was the prisoner's wife; he was present, and did not deny it.
HENRY YORKE BRACE. I am a bookseller. Twenty-six of Dolby's Plays were brought to me on the 10th of March, by a woman, who appears since to be the prisoner's wife; she came to me again, and I gave information to the prosecutor.
JOHN CUMBERLAND. I live on Ludgate-hill, and am a bookseller - I have a warehouse in Play-house-yard, and the prisoner was my warehouse-man, and had the whole care of that warehouse. I received some information, and employed an officer to watch his proceedings. On the 18th of March I went with the officer to Play-house-yard, and remained at some distance, while he took the prisoner down a gate way, to search him - these articles are all mine - I never gave him any perquisites. I can swear to these Percy Aneedotes, by a blank place being left for the name.
Prisoner. Q. Were not a great number of these plates and books lying under foot in the warehouse for any one to take? A. No; I employed you to take them to the warehouse, and put them away - they are now in a course of sale, and have been so these four or five years.
GUILTY. Aged 43.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-305]
878. FREDERICK EDWARDS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Louisa Rankins, on the 18th of March, putting her in fear, and taking from her person and against her will, 2 shillings, her monies.
LOUISA RANKINS. I am single. On the evening of the 18th of March I saw the prisoner in Holborn, and went with him to a house in Gloucester-court - we were both very much in liquor - we went into a bed room, but I did not go to bed, because I did not wish it; I do not know whether I was on the bed or not, nor whether he did any thing to me or no - the landlord came up while he was there, and I believe the prisoner struck me; I lost a tooth but I do not know who gave me the blow; I had some money in my hand, which was taken or knocked out, but I do not know who did it - the watchman came in at the time, but I do not know whether the prisoner did any thing when he came - we were both quite drunk.
ROBERT EVANS. I am a watchman. I was called to this house by a female; I found the prisoner and the prosecutrix in the two-pair back room - her mouth was bleeding very much - she was crying; I was informed, by a female, that the man had been beating this girl - she said"He has robbed me, as well as beat me, and I hope you will take him;" the prisoner said it served her right, and we secured him. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-306]
879. WILLIAM BLACKETT and ROBERT LYALL were indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of John Emmerson, on the night of the 26th of February, and stealing 2 gowns, value 5s.; 2 tippets, value 6d.; 2 shifts, value 4s.; 2 night gowns, value 1s.; 1 pelisse, value 15s.; 1 shirt, value 1s.; 1 locket, value 6d., and 1 ring, value 6d., his property.
JANE PERKINS. I live at Mr. Hemming's, in Foster-street, City, and keep a school. On Sunday the 26th of February I went out between twelve and one o'clock at noon - I locked my door - I was sent for about eight, and found these articles on a table ready to be taken away - they were locked in a drawer when I went out; a locket, a ring, and a brooch, were gone; I have not seen them since.
Cross-examined by Mr. BARRY. Q. Do you know the prisoner? A. No.
RICHARD EDWARDS. I was in Foster-street on the 26th of February, between six and seven o'clock in the evening - I saw Blacket at one corner of the street, and Bailey putting a key into the door of No. 3 - Lyall stood before him; I went up the street and saw Sawyer standing at the corner - I and another boy went and played in the Broadway for two or three minutes; we then went down the street again; I saw Sawyer go down and whistle - they then all went from the door - Sawyer went down the street and came to the corner again; Blacket and the others then went away - we went down again and saw the door open, which had been shut when we passed it before; we went to Walton, the officer, and told him - he came with us to the house - as we were coming down the street we saw the prisoners coming up together - Sawyer was behind them - they were coming from No. 3; we went down to the house - the officer went into the passage and said"Who is here;" the door was then opened, and Bailey said "It is me, Sir;" Walton caught hold of his collar, he wrestled in the road and got away, but left his hat behind; we found the drawers open in Miss Perkins' front parlour, and the things on the ground.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Sawyer? A. Yes; by working at Mr. Page's; I am an apprentice to Mr. Law, in the New Market, but since last October I have been living with my mother, as I did not like the business; I have been there eight or nine months - my master is willing to take me back - he has complained of me in a Court of Justice, and I was sent to the House of Correction; I once had part of the money for which a watch was sold, but I did not take it; I believe I have run away from my master about six times; I was once taken out of a lodging house, but I do not know that it was a house of ill-fame.
COURT. Q. Upon your oath did you go to meet any girl or woman there? A. No, it was a lodging-house for men - I do not know who kept it - I was there only one night - I slept by myself - I went there about the watch concern because I was afraid to go home.
THOMAS SHROPSHIRE. I was in Foster-street with Edwards - I saw Blacket at the corner of Half-moon-alley; it was then between half-past six and seven o'clock - we went on and saw Bailey standing, at the door of No. 3, making a noise with something, and Lyall was behind him on the curb - we went on and saw Sawyer standing at the top of the street - we went to the Broadway, and played at a step, and then we went down again, and saw them at the same place - we then went down on the same side as the house was, and saw Lyall and Bailey, two or three yards off the door - Blacket was at the same place as at first - we went round Peter-street, and came down again and saw Bailey and Lyall at the same place, and the door was open, which had been shut before; Sawyer then went down Peter-street; we went round again, and they all went different ways; we went to the officer - he went with us into Peter-street, and saw Blacket and Lyall just by a gas light in the same street, some distance off - we saw Sawyer come by, and we went to the house, and the officer knocked at the shutters, and said "Who is here?" Bailey came out, and said "I am;" the officer collared him- he struggled and got away - I went into Miss Perkins' room - I found the candle wrapped round with a black stocking, and these things about the floor - the lock of the drawer seemed to be broken.
Cross-examined. Q. Bailey was the man who came out of the house? A. Yes; I did not know them before but I can identify their persons; I am a companion of Edwards' - we had been buying some sugar - candy, and were going home.
JOSEPH WALTON. I am the officer. These boys fetched me to Foster-street; I saw Blacket and Lyall coming up the street about two yards from the corner, as I turned into the street, about ten or twelve yards from the house - I then went to No. 3, and saw Bailey - I struggled with him and he escaped; I found the drawers open, these things on the ground, and a pick-lock key in the street-door.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the two witnesses? Q. Yes; I had Edwards in custody from his master for running away - but he liberated him again; I did not know they were the parties till after I found them out - I knew them well, and could not be deceived in them; the boys had told me a house had been broken open, but did not say who were doing it.
The prisoners called -
WILLIAM LAWS. I am a boot-maker, and live in East-street, Finsbury-market. Edwards was apprenticed to my father - after being a week on liking he ran away; we found he was connected with a notorious set of young thieves - my father put him into the House of Correction; he was with us, in the whole, fifteen or sixteen months, and ran away fifteen or sixteen times; he has been taken from a lodging-house in Chick-lane, and from the Royalty Theatre; he has been out three or four days and nights together, and seldom came home without being fetched.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-307]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
880. JOHN JEFFERS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 6ozs. weight of tea, value 9d., the goods of the United Company of Merchants, trading to the East Indies .
Mr. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
JOHN LINES. I am in the employ of the East India Company. I was employed to watch at their warehouse in Crutched-friars, where some men were mending chests - they went to breakfast at ten o'clock, and soon after the prisoner came into the cellar and went to a chest - I heard his hand go into the chest three distinct times; I then went for Mr. Frazer, the elder, and said I must search the prisoner; he said he had not got any thing - but after I took him down to the counting-house he offered me a sovereign to let go his hand; I found this tea in his breeches pocket - the chest had been plundered before - he had no business in the cellar.
ALEXANDER FRAZER. I am an elder. I saw the prisoner - Lines searched him in my presence, and about six ounces of black tea was taken from his left-hand breeches pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. I took a book from George Williams - I went to tell him he had brought a wrong number, and I asked him to change it. GUILTY. Aged 22.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-308]
881. JOHN WEAKEIN was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of February, 4 ozs. weight of tea, value 1s., the goods of the United Company of Merchants, trading to the East Indies .
GEORGE TIMEWELL. On Monday, the 27th of February, I was at the East India Company's sale-room, at Cutler-street - I am commodore of that warehouse. The prisoner came into the room, and asked for some samples of tea - he had six breaks of Hyson tea set before him, and he took samples of them all - it is usual for persons to bring samples of the same sort of tea as they intend to buy; I was so placed in the room, that I could see exactly what he did - he was sampling the tea, that is, taking a small quantity from each, in a little measure like the top of a cannister, which is the limit of the quantity allowed to be taken away; there are rules stuck upon the show-room, that no persons but such as are known are to have samples, and that none of them are to have more than the in measure; as the prisoner was sampling the teas, the man who was attending to him, went to get some more from another break, and I saw the prisoner, in his absence, take some of the tea, and put it loose into his right-hand small clothes pocket - he did this several times; I told Mr. French, the elder, he told me to watch him again, and not to accuse him without good reason; I then saw him put some Hyson tea into his breast-pocket; Mr. French then desired him to walk into the counting-house, and the tea was found on him; Mr. French said, "It is a pity a gentleman like you should act in such a way;" he seemed to be quite cut up about it, and begged Mr. French's pardon, and hoped he would let him off, and not take any further notice. Mr. French said he must tell the warehouse-keeper, and he might do as he thought proper - he said he had been a long while out of work, and was very much distressed.
Prisoner. Q. Were there a number of persons in the sale room? A. Yes; I was kneeling down, your face was towards me, and your back to the corner; I cannot state what breaks of tea you took most out of - you had about sixteen trays handed to you.
COURT. Q. You saw what was going on, and informed the elder? A. Yes - the prisoner was still at the tray - when he had done he got up to go away - he took his bag and went down stairs - the elder was at the bottom, and stopped him.
WILLIAM FRENCH. I am assistant elder to the Company. There were from twenty to thirty persons nailing up chests - when the prisoner came in, the commodore came to me, and said, "That gentleman has put some tea into his pockets - I desired him to watch him more closely, and I went down - when he came down stairs, and was going into the yard, I told him to walk into the counting-house, and said, I was very sorry to say he had got some tea - I had ordered the excise-officer to be there, and the tea was found upon him - he first said, "We were very short of samples," and then said, "I am very much distressed, and I did it for myself, being out of a situation."
Prisoner. Q. Will you swear you did not come into the room where I was, and say, "Before you go I want to speak to you"? A. No - I never once spoke to you, nor came near to you - the business was never communicated to you till you came down.
COURT. Q. When people come to buy teas, do they bring samples and leave them there? A. Yes; the prisoner brought six samples, and I counted six samples which he had in his bag - the tea in his pockets was beyound the samples he had brought.
Prisoner. Q. When you asked me if I had any tea in my breeches pocket, I said I had - you said I must leave it - and did I not give it out? A. Yes; I took up your coat pocket and turned it out - you made no resistance.
WILLIAM BATEMAN. I was the Excise officer. I was present when the tea was found in his breeches and his side coat pockets; French took it from him, and said he had understood he had some tea - he trembled very much, and said he had, and he hoped Mr. French would not do any thing in it - he said he came from Messrs. Whitridge and Co., No. 91, Cheapside; I did not hear him say any thing about his own situation.
Prisoner. Q. Were you not in the room when I sampled these teas? A. No.
Prisoner's Defence. I was sampling the tea for some friends, having a great many friends, and having been twenty years in the business. I sampled a number of breaks of Hyson, and the sixteenth and seventeenth breaks appeared to me to be of a particular quality, and I took some of it. I do not deny taking it, but I do deny its being with a felonious intent; the last witness was at the end of the table, and twenty or thirty men were nailing down tea chests - and was it likely I should have gone to commit a felony at two o'clock in broad day. When I was asked if I had not tea I immediately gave it up.
COURT to WILLIAM FRENCH. Q. What did he say? A. I said, "How came you to take this?" and he said, "I am short of samples;" and then he said it was for himself.
JURY. Q. Is it customary to allow the parties to take their samples themselves, or is it given them by the officer of the Company? A. He takes the sample out in a tin measure - it is put into a paper, and the sample they bring is measured in the same measure.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-309]
London Cases, Second Jury.
882. DONALD MEREDITH was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 5 pieces of paper, stamped with certain stamps of 5s. each, value 25s.; 4 other pieces, with 4s. 6d. stamps, value 18s., and 4 pieces with 3s. 6d. stamps, value 14s., the goods of James Smith and Thomas Shirley; and JOSEPH ZAMIRA was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well-knowing them to have been stolen.
Mr. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution
THOMAS SHIRLEY. I am in partnership with James Smith - we are wine-merchants, and live in Crescent-place, Bridge-street, Blackfriars. The prisoner Meredith came sometimes to our counting-house, to see his brother, who is in my employ. We have a number of stamps for use in our business, and I very frequently missed some. I accordingly put a private mark on those left in my drawer - they were 3s. 6d., 4s. 6d., 5s., 6s., and 8s. 6d. bill stamps. I made a small mark in the stamp, by which I should know them from all others. From five o'clock in the evening of the 13th of March, till nine in the morning of the 16th, I missed a considerable number - on the evening of the 17th I missed some more; Meredith had been atmy office that evening, and in consequence of something said by me to his brother, he was sent out, and returned; I saw him in the street, and he was taken to an opposite office - I asked him for the stamps - he denied having any, but I insisted upon having them, and said I had no doubt he had them on his person, and I would search him unless he would give them up - he then gave them up. I took him to my house in Chatham-place, and afterwards to the Mansion House. In consequence of information he gave us we went that evening to Zamira's house, in Duke's-place, but did not go in; we went there again on the evening of the 18th, and part of my stamps were found there. I never sell stamps in any way whatever - here are what I found on the prisoner Meredith when he was taken, and here are those found at Zamira's on the 18th - they are what I had lost on the 13th or 14th.
Cross-examined by Mr. ALLEY. Q. Then it was not on the evening you took the boy you went to Zamira? A. No; we did not go into the house till the evening of the 18th; I have about six servants; the stamps were not locked up - they were in a private counting-house; Mereditch could have access to it, because he came at eight o'clock in the morning, before the clerks came; we have three porters and two clerks, but the counting-house is never left by myself or Mr. Smith; I think only one clerk or book-keeper knew where the stamps were kept.
WILLIAM MERCHANT. I am an officer. I went to Zamira's house, at the corner of Bury-street, St. Mary Axe, on the 17th, and on the 18th I went in with the prisoner Meredith, Mr. Shirley, and a gentleman here - we sent Meredith in, who asked if Zamira was at home; they said Yes; as I went in he was coming down stairs; I said to him "Do you know this young man," (pointing to Meredith) who was standing with his back against the door - he said No; there was no other young man in the shop; I asked what he had done with the stamps he bought of Meredith; he said he knew nothing at all about them; I then told him I was an officer; he said "Don't make a noise, I have got my house full of company;" he then spoke to his wife, or an elderly woman there, and, to the best of my recollection, it was to go up stairs, and bring the stamps down; she brought them down and gave them to Zamira - he gave them to Mr. Shirley, who identified them as his property; here are four for 3s. 6d., four for 4s. 6d., and five for 5s.; they are quite clean, and here is a mark on every one of them; we then came down to the Mansion-house; they were then committed - Meredith did not deny it or say any thing about the stamps produced - it is a kind of grocer's and stationer's shop I understand.
Cross-examined by Mr. BRODRICK. Q. Did he call to this old lady? A. No; she came without being called - he only said "Go and fetch me the stamps;" I heard all he said - he is not deaf that I know of.
PETER MURPHY. I am an officer. I went to Zamira on the 17th, and again on the 18th, but I did not go in till the stamps were delivered.
WILLIAM NORWOOD. I am cellarman to Messrs. Smith and Shirley. Persons come there to business at a quarter or half-past seven o'clock in the morning; I did not know before the 18th of March, where the stamps were; I have seen Meredith about the house in a morning - he used to go to the counting-house when his brother was not there; I saw him about a week or ten days before he was apprehended.
THOMAS SHIRLEY. These are a part of the stamps which I marked on the 13th or 14th of March.
Cross-examined by Mr. BRODRICK. Q. What is the value of those bits of paper without the stamps? A. A halfpenny or a penny.
MEREDITH's Defence. Mr. Shirley gave me a promise that if I would tell all I knew he would do nothing to me.
Nine witnesses gave Zamira an excellent character.
MEREDITH - GUILTY. Aged 17.
ZAMIRA - GUILTY. Aged 56.
Recommended to Mercy.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-310]
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
883. ANTHONY DRUMADA was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 1 hat, value 5s., the goods of Robert Hugh Franks.
JAMES BAYLEY. I am in the employ of Robert Hugh Franks, a hatter, of Red Cross-street, Barbican. On the 18th of March, a witness, named Phelps, told me some person had taken a hat from the door - I went out and received this hat from a young man who had been in the shop buying one. The prisoner was then brought back.(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM PHELPS. I am fourteen years of age. I was in Red-cross-street on the 18th of March, about half-past five o'clock - I saw the prisoner take the hat from a peg - he said to me "Do not say any thing - come along with me;" he went a good way off with it - I did not see him stopped - I told the shopman of it.
WILLIAM PAYNE. I am an officer. I was sent for to take the prisoner into custody - I found him in the shop, and this sack under his arm - the hat was given me by the witness - I took him to the Compter, and found four half-crowns and 4s. 6d. in his pockets.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to buy a hat, and a man gave me a push, and told me to go on; I had the hat, and some men came and took me by the collar; I had the bag to go to Billingsgate to get a job - the hat was outside the door.
JAMES BAYLEY re-examined. The hat was on a stand inside the door - a young man there saw him take it - he followed and brought him back.
GUILTY. Aged 55.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18260406-311]
884. JOHN KELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Lowndes, from his person.
THOMAS LOWNDES. I was in Fleet-street, near Water-lane, on the 5th of April; I felt something press behind me, and put my hand to my pocket, and my handkerchief was gone; I turned, and the prisoner was as close behind me as he could be; I said he had taken the handkerchief, and I saw it between his feet - there were two youths behind him - he denied having taken it.
Prisoner. I saw two lads before him, and one just off the curb; he turned round and said to me he had lost his handkerchief; I said I knew nothing about it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-312]
885. WILLIAM DRAPER was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am a fishmonger, and live in Leadenhall-market. The prisoner was employed by me to receive money. On the 7th of March he was sent to receive 10l. 17s. 6d.; he never returned to me, nor accounted to me for it; I saw him again the next night - he did jobs about the market - I had employed him at that time.
JOHN STALKER. The prisoner called on me on the 7th of March, and asked for some money for Mr. Brown; I gave him a cheque for 10l., a half-sovereign, and seven shillings - he did not give a receipt.
ROGER JONATHAN SEABROOK. I am a clerk at Messrs. Hanbury and Co's.; I paid this cheque on the 7th of March.
GEORGE WATTS. I am an officer. I was informed that the prisoner had absconded, and went to Brunswick-place, New Kent-road, at nine o'clock at night, on the 8th of March - I found him in bed - there was a woman and child in the room; he delivered to Knight 9l. 13s. 6d.; I asked what he had done with the cheque; he said he had changed it, and laid some out in a pair of shoes, a shirt, and a pair of stockings.
COURT to WILLIAM BROWN. Q. How did you know he lived over the water? A. I received information from a young lad in the market; he had before that lived a long while in Shoreditch, with his mother; I have known him fifteen years; I thought him an honest lad
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy. Confined Six Month.
[trialAccount t18260406-313]
886. CHARLES MACARTNEY was indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of John Warwick, on the night of the 26th of February, and stealing 3 ozs. of gold value 12l.; 15 ozs. of gold and silver mixed, value 15l., 2 pieces of silver, value 1s.; 1 piece of a spoon, value 2s.; 8 ozs. of silver plate, value 50s., and 1 sovereign, the property of the said John Warwick and others his partners.
GEORGE THOMAS SIMPSON. I am in the employ of Mr. John Warwick - he has two partners - they are surgeons, and he is a gold refiner - they have a share in the gold refining concern. On Saturday, the 25th of February, I locked up their iron chest, about six o'clock in the evening; this gold and silver was in it; I had bought the piece of silver, just a week before; two pieces of massy silver I had bought on the 25th of Otton; I had put them into the fire and melted them in the kitchen, by his request - they came out in a particular shape, and I can identify them - the chest stood in the counting-house, which was never locked; I went into the counting-house again on Monday morning, about seven o'clock; it joins the workshop by a passage; part of the workshop is tiled in; I saw a large hole in the tiles of the workshop, enough to admit a man's body; I had not been there on Sunday - I only passed through the passage to go into the house; I then went into the counting house, the door of which was open, and I found the iron chest open which I had left locked, and missed the gold and silver, and the two massy pieces of silver; I then missed a sovereign from the till of the counter in the counting-house, which I had seen there on the Saturday night; I had locked the till, and had the key; the lock had been forced open; the lock of the chest appeared to have been opened with the key which had been in one of the partitions of the till, where the sovereign was.
Cross-examined by Mr. BARRY. Q. Was that the only money in the till? A. No, there was 3s. in halfpence, and they were taken too. The surgery business is not carried on under the same roof - they do not live there; one of the prosecutor's brothers went away on the night of the robbery - the other I saw a fortnight ago; I know that he was acquainted with the prisoner, and that he is not now to be found - the counting-house is part of the dwelling-house.
BENJAMIN WEBB. I am constable of St. James. Westminster. I went to the prisoner's lodgings on Tuesday, the 28th of February, at No. 5, Bow-yard, Drury-lane, and found him in bed - I told him I wanted him on a charge of felony; he dressed himself, and I searched him- he refused me at first, and I observed him put his hand into his left-hand breeches pocket, and draw out something, which he dropped on the bed. I then tied his hands with a handkerchief, and looked on the bed, where I found three pieces of silver - I took charge of them immediately. I searched the room, and found a pair of shoes - the Magistrate asked at the office if they were his, and he acknowledged that they were. I went two days afterwards to the prosecutor's workshop - I observed the opening, and saw the print of a footstep ont the chimney, within half a yard of the opening; I fitted the right shoe to the footsteps - it appeared to correspond exactly - I found in the prisoner's room one new and some old crucibles, and a basin with some sort of metal in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you search his pockets before he dressed himself? A. No. I compared one of the shoes with the footsteps, but I did not mention that at the first examination. Mr. Warwick's brother said he did not know where he was; I took a deal of trouble to find him, but I could not. I found some property of Mr. Warwick's brother's at the prisoner's lodging.
THOMAS MORIAH OTTON. I am an engine-turner. On the 25th of February I sold Mr. Simpson those pieces of silver - they were in one piece; I begged he would put them into the fire, to ascertain their quality; it is in the same shape as when he brought it from the fire - they are worth about 1s., and this piece of spoon is worth 1s.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean it has the same surface that it had then? A. Yes.
COURT to BENJAMIN WEBB. Q. Did he say how he got the silver, before the Magistrate? A. Yes - he first said that he found them in Peter's pocket, and then said Peter gave them to him.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 27th of February Peter,(the prosecutor's brother,) came to my place before breakfast, and gave me 6s. to fetch a coat from the dyer's, that wanted repairing; I repaired it for him - he said he wanted to meet a friend, and he could not go in his fustian jacket - he pulled off the jacket, and put on the coat; I removed the jacket, and the three pieces of silver fell out- he has never been found since.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Of stealing to the value of 39s. only, and not of burglary.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-314]
887. WILLIAM GOODMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 3 pieces of paper, each being stamped with a stamp, denoting the payment of a duty of 1s. 9d., value 1s. 9d. each, and 21 other pieces of stamped paper, value 1s. 3d. each, the goods of Charles Cooper, John Henry Cooper, and William Cooper.
The prisoner pleaded. GUILTY.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18260406-315]
888. CHARLES MORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of March, 1 iron bound cask, value 7s. 6d., 10 gallons and 1 quart of noyeau, value 5l. 12s. 6d., the goods of Francis Pathonier and Maria Weller.
WILLIAM FAIRBAIRN. I am clerk to Francis Pathonier and Maria Weller, distillers, of Silver-street, Wood-street. I know this cask to be their property - there were ten gallons and a quart of noyeau in it.
THOMAS STYLES. I am carman to the prosecutors. On the 3d of March I left the cart at the corner of Angel-street - when I came back this cask was gone; I had left it on the cart about three minutes and a half before; there had been seven in the cart, and this laid behind, on the near side, by the tail board.
THOMAS HUGHES. I was at the corner of St. Ana's-lane, where I saw a cart, with the name of Pathonier and Weller on it - the carman was gone up the street, and I saw a man get into the cart, and deliver this cask out to the prisoner - the other weat towards Newgate-street; the prisoner went towards Bull and Mouth-street. I and several others stopped him, and asked where he was going with it - he said a man had ordered him to take it to a place just by; he then put down the cask, but we made him bring it back. I went and got the officer.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not see the man come and ask me if I would carry a load for him? A. No. - there were five of you together.
GEORGE THOMPSON. I am a constable. I took the prisoner and cask to Pathonier and Wellers.
Prisoner's Defence. A man came and asked me to carry this load; I said Yes, and he got into the cart.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-316]
889. HENRY CADE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of Edward Lodge Ogle, from his person.
EDWARD LODGE OGLE. On the 5th of April I was in Gracechurch-street; I felt my pocket touched, turned round, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand - I saw him drop it. I took him to the Mansion House. I had it in my pocket an instant before.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along Gracechurch-street - there was a sailor and another young man behind this gentleman, and directly he turned round they turned, and dropped the handkerchief - I took it up, and was going to put it into my pocket, when he took me.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-317]
890. WILLIAM DADE and JOHN GOODHALL were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of March, 42lbs. of solder, value 20s.; 1 sucker for a pump, value 6d., and 7 wall-hooks, value 6d., the goods of Robert Hardcastle and Sarah Dunn.
JOHN DUNN. My mother, Sarah Dunn, is in partnership with Robert Harcastle. The prisoners had been in their employ seven or eight months; between five and seven o'clock on the evening of the 4th of March, I gave Goodhall the key of the solder cupboard - I then took it away, and said I had not time to lock to it, but in ten minutes after I gave him the key again to go down to the solder cupboard - he went down, with Dade and Hedley - Goodhall was below, and Dade at the top, and Hedley was handing the solder to the counting-house - I stood over the cellar looking at them - I waited for the key; when they had got it all up, I said, "Is it all up?" they said,"Yes;" I then hung up the key and went to my tea - on my return I found all the men waiting, except those three - I went after them, and found them still in the cellar - I jumped down, then Dade ran from them and I called him, but he would not come - I called Goodhall to give me a light, and I held it to Dade, and said, "Is that you?" I took him into the counting-house - he said "It is my first offence;" I said, "You have been found guilty before, but forgiven;" I sent Goodhall for an officer - he was gone some time, and in the mean time the officer came; they were taken to the watch-house - I was present when they were searched; this new sucker was found on Dade, which I believe to be my mother's - and here are seven wall-books, which I cannot swear to - but we had articles of this description; here is half of a sucker, which was found on Goodhall; this solder was found on Dade, under his coat, when I went into the cellar - it is of the same description as that they handed out of the cellar - these four pieces were secreted away, and found the following morning.
Cross-examined by Mr. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you know of their being in the cellar? A. Not till I came down; it was necessary to clean the cellar - they went down to get coals and coke up; I found these two bars of solder on Dade - they fit our mould exactly - we make them ourselves; others may have moulds of the same shape; Hedley had the making of the suckers - it should have been hanging up - he ought to have shown it to me, but he had not; it fits the mould on which he was working.
Prisoner DADE. Q. Will you say the solder was secreted? A. It was carried across his arm.
ROBERT HARDCASTLE. These two men were in our employ - I was in the counting-house when they came up - the witness brought Dade in to me, and said,"I have found out the thieves;" I began to remonstrate with him; he at first said he was going to bring it up stairs, and then he said, he hoped I would forgive him - he said he would make me any amends - I found these other pieces of solder hid upon some old wood where we deposit Coke.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you mean by hid? A. They were in a place where they had no business to be - there was nothing on them; I stood on tip too - the candle glanced on them; Dade said he was going to bring up the solder - Goodhall was not present; it is the custom to keep a certain quantity in the counting house; Dads did not say he had been told to bring that up to the counting-house - what he said was, that he was going to bring it up stairs.
COURT to JOHN DUNN. You mentioned that these men were gone into the cellar to fetch coals and coke - but is that usual? A. No; the labourers should do it, but they will do as they like - they do fetch them sometimes; they had the coal basket in their hands.
ABRAHAM GRIFFITHS. I am a constable. I searched the two prisoners at the watch-house; I found on Dade seven wall-hooks and the sucker - and on Goodhall, a little bit of clack.
DADE's Defence. I had no intention of stealing the solder; I had it given me by Goodhall - and I was bringing it up when Mr. Dunn stopped me.
GOODHALL'S Defence. This solder I was ordered to get up: I returned the keys, and in the course of the evening Mr. Dunn told us to go and get coals and coke up; when I had delivered the keys to Mr. Dunn, he came running down, and went up to Dade, and said, "What have you got?" he said, "Two bars of metal to take up stais;" he called to me to get the candle, which I did - and when I came up he told me to go and get a constable, which I did.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-318]
SEVENTH DAY. THURSDAY, APRIL 13.
London Cases, Second Jury.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant
891. JOHN EDWARD OAKLEY, alias JOHN EDWARDS , was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Mary Charles, widow, about the hour of eight in the night of the 4th of March, at St. Botolph, Aldersgate, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 6 child's shirts, value 2s.; 3 petticoats, value 1s.; 1 frock, value 1s.; 8 caps, value 1s. 6d.; 4 shirts, value 1l. 2s.; 5 neckerchiefs, value 7s.; 3 pairs of stockings, value 4s. 6d.; 7 handkerchiefs, value 11s.; 3 towels, value 1s.; three gowns, value 7s. 6d.; 9 aprons, value 9s.; 1 cloak, value 10s.; 1 pair of sheets, value 2s. 6d.; 1 table-cloth, value 1s. 6d.; 1 shift, value 1s.; 1 shawl, value 1s., and 1 curtain, value 1s. 6d., her property.
MARY CHARLES. I am a widow, and live at No. 5, Cox's-court, Aldersgate-street, in the parish of St. Botolph without, Aldersgate. I rent the bottom part of the house, and have the key of that part - the landlord does not live in the house - it is all let out in tenements. I am a washerwoman. I went out on the 4th of March, at near eight o'clock in the evening; I left the property stated in the indictment there, some on my table, some in my drawer; and some in a basket. I returned in about twenty minutes; I had locked my door when I went out, and on my return I found the articles were all gone; this is the property (examining it) - it is all mine.
ROBERT LOCK. I am a constable. On Saturday evening, the 4th of March, about twenty minutes past eight o'clock, I was in Whitecross-street, and saw the prisoner coming down, with two bundles, one under each arm; this was about half a mile from Cox's-court - I called an officer, named Bradford, and we stopped him, and asked what he had got - he said he had just come from his mother's, in Hare-court, and he met a man, who sad he would give him a shilling to carry these two bundles; I asked where he was - he said at the Merry Carpenters public-house, in Old-street. I told him I would give him every chance to find the man, and went to the house with him, but he was not to be found. I then locked him up, and in consequence of taking another man afterwards, with some kid skins, who told me I might find some more skins in a house in Gloucester-court, Whitecross-street, I went there about ten o'clock that night, and found some more property belonging to the prosecutrix. The whole are not more than one person could carry.
Prisoner. Had not he a right to take the woman into custody where the property was found? A. Witness. There was a woman frying some fish there, and I saw a trunk open - I looked at the things in it, and saw some wet mud on them, which excited my suspicion. I took them, but I did not know they were stolen - they are a part of this property.
Prisoner. The reason he did not is, because Harris, her brother-in-law, is an officer, who was with him. Witness. Yes - I called him to go with me.
THOMAS BRADFORD. I was with hock, and took this man and the things - when we got to the watch-house it was about half-past eight o'clock.
COURT to MARY CHARLES. Q. Tell us as nearly as you can the time you left you house? A. It was just upon the stroke of eight o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence (written). Between eight and nine o'clock at night I met a tall man in Redcross-street, with three bundles under his arm - he said if I would carry two of them to the Merry Carpenters, in Old-street, he would give me 1s.
Two witnesses gave the prisoner an excellent character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 10.
[trialAccount t18260406-319]
892. SARAH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 1 fowl, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Loveday.
JAMES THOMAS SELWOOD. I am a salesman in Newgate-market. I saw the prisoner lurking about my shop for some hours; I then saw her go to Mr. Thomas Loveday's shop, and take a shawl off a hamper - she secreted it under her apron. I went and charged her with it; on her being removed the fowl was found under her; she had been close to the place, but when I spoke to her she had got two or three yards from it.
Prisoner's Defence. I knocked it down with my shawl.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18260406-320]
893. JOHN TIPPENDEN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 rummer glass, value 1s., the goods of John Kingston.
GEORGE REED. I am an officer. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house on the 28th of March, and Mr. John Kingston gave me this glass which the prisoner confessed he had taken, and begged forgiveness - he said he was in a state of intoxication, and did not know what he had done; there were two other glasses found on him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-321]
894. MICHAEL LARNER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 printed bound book, value 8s., the goods of Thomas Bumpus.
THOMAS BUMPUS. I live at Holborn-bars, in the City. I am a bookseller - this book is mine; I had seen it safe about twelve o'clock on the 1st of April.
JOSEPH NICHOLSON. I am porter at the Blue Posts public-house. On the 1st of April Mrs. Bumpus called to me, about ten minutes past two o'clock, to go after the prisoner - she gave me his description; I found him in Middle-row, looking into a bookseller's shop; I brought him back, and found this book in his right hand coat pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. I cannot say I am not guilty, but the officer can tell you I was in a desperate state of madness, occasioned by a kick of a horse over my eye; I had drank some liquor that day; I was tied down in a straight waistcoat for thirty hours in convulsive fits in consequence of what I had done.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18260406-322]
895. THOMAS PERKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 wooden chest, value 2s. 6d., and 50lbs. weight of tea, value 13l. 15s. the goods of Thomas Conway and others, his partners.
CHARLES TOWNSEND. I am warehouseman to Thomas Conway and others - they are wholesale grocers and tea dealers, and live in Maiden-lane, Queen-street, City. On Thursday last, the 6th of April, I loaded the cart with one chest of tea, one bag of tea, and some sugars; I gave it in charge of Blunt, the carman, about half-past eleven o'clock.
JOSEPH BLUNT. I am the carman. I received this chest of tea in my cart on Thursday last; I drove it along Bishopsgate-street, and there I stopped; I was about forty yards behind the cart when I saw the prisoner go and move the chest of tea from the side to the tail - he then turned round and lifted it over the chain, took it on his shoulder, and walked away; I ran and caught him about half-way across the road.
THOMAS WHITFIELD. I was sent down to Liverpool-street; I met the carman, who called and asked me to have something to drink - we were going to have it, when he turned round and said "I have a chest of tea in my cart, and I must have a sharp eye on it;" he then saw the prisoner taken it and ran after him; I saw him drop the chest, which Blunt gave me charge of, and he took the prisoner - the chest was close at his heels.
WILLIAM RILEY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner and the tea.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along Bishopsgate-street, and a man asked me if I wanted a job; I said Yes; he said "If you will take this chest of tea to the Catherine Wheel, I will pay for it."
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18260406-323]
896. WILLIAM PRYER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 8lbs. of beef, value 1s., the goods of George Lee.
GEORGE LEE. I am a salesman at Newgate-market. I lost a piece of beef from a book in my shop; I had seen it about ten minutes before it was taken; I saw it with a carcass of lamb which the prisoner had purchased in the morning - he paid 2s. on it; he came in the afternoon to pay the remaing 17s. 6d.; I told my man to have an eye on him - he told me he had taken a shin of beef; I ran out and saw the prisoner with the carcass of lamb, and a cloth wrapped over it - the beef was in the carcass.
Cross-examined by Mr. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you see how it got into the carcass? A. No; it is a small piece, but it could not have fallen into it, because the beef hung under the lamb - it required some force to get it in.
WILLIAM PRENTICE. I am Lee's clerk. On the 7th of April I saw the prisoner take a shin of beef off a book, and put it inside the carcass of lamb; I ran in and told Mr. Lee- the prisoner had a cloth which wrapped round the lamb and the beef in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not usual to have a cloth? A. Yes; I told Mr. Lee the instant I saw him do it.
HENRY KELLY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, who was very outrageous; he said he would not go to the Compter - he clung round a post, and I was forced to use a little force with him.
Prisoner's Defence. I understood the scale-man in the morning that he would hang the lamb in the back-yard, and I went there for it; the young man said it was on the right side, and he came and took the note off it; I took it on my shoulder, and some of the shins of beef fell down, one fell into the lamb, and one fell down - the man then caught the lamb and said I wanted to rob him; I said I had no idea of it; I thought it was only a joke; I then went into the market, and came back to Mr. Lee, who was talking with some gentleman; they said "Poh, Poh, nonsense!"
WILLIAM PARSONS. I am a cheesemonger and grocer, and live in Catherine-street, Limehouse-fields; I have employed the prisoner two months, and he has always given me satisfaction, and laid out my money honestly; I was with him the day in question, when he paid the 2s. off the carcass, which he bought for me; when I missed him I called on Mr. Lee, and asked if he knew any thing of a man who had paid him 2s. off a carcass of lamb - he said he had been there, and attempted to steal a shin of beef, and he had got him safe enough, and if I would stand 2l. he would not appear against him; I would take him into my employ immediately.
GEORGE LEE. He said the man had a wife and four children - I said I would not mention any thing but what related to his occurrence, though he has done several bad things.
GUILTY. Aged 44.
Recommended to Mercy, on account of his character.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18260406-324]
897. JOSEPH NIGHTINGALE was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January, 1 watch, value 1l. 12s.; 1 seal, value 12s. 6d.; 1 ring, value 1d., and 1 watch key, value 6d., the goods of William Charlton Wright.
WILLIAM CHARLTON WRIGHT. I keep the Dolphin chop-house, on Ludgate-hill . The prisoner used my house in January last - one day in that month I wrote a letter at the table, by the fire, and the prisoner was in a box at a short distance from me. I took out my watch, and put it on the table, while I used my seal - I turned round to send my servant with the letter, and in that timethe prisoner had gone out, and I immediately missed my watch, with the seal attached to it. I have not seen him since, but Mrs. Wright says he has been in the house - he had been there several times before.
Cross-examined by Mr. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had not there been some degree of intimacy between you? A. Nothing further than his being a customer. I saw he had plenty of money, and I once asked him to lend me a 5l. note - he showed me an acceptance for 50l., and asked if I could make use of that - I kept it an hour, and then returned it; I never talked about money on any other occasion - he owed me money for eating and drinking at my house; I knew his address, and wrote him two letters, which he answered - the Lord Mayor has them. The letters found him in Bride-lane, Fleet-street; I called there twice, and could not see him; I had not given orders for his apprehension - he was taken in about two months - if I had given instructions for his being taken before there can be no doubt he would have been taken.
COURT. Q. How long was it before he took the watch that you had talked about the 5l.? A. It might be about a fortnight. I did not see him take the watch, and he did not ask my leave - he never denied having taken it.
JOSEPH MARTIN. I apprehended the prisoner on the 5th of April - he gave me a key, which he said belonged to his box, at his lodgings, at the Talbot Inn, in the Borough, and if I went there I should find a pocket-book, with the duplicate of the watch in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you see him? A. At the corner of Swithin's-lane - I told him what I took him for, and he said if I would go with him to the prosecutor he would soon make it all right.
ROBERT HESKETH. The last witness gave me a key of a box, in which I found a pocket-book, and this duplicate in it.
WILLIAM STAPLE. I am a pawnbroker. The watch was pawned with me, by the prisoner, on the 31st of January.
Prisoner's Defence. I never took it with any felonious intention, which my prosecutor is, I believe, well aware.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18260406-325]
376. JOSEPH NIGHTINGALE was again indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 1 silver snuff-box, value 30s., the goods of Thomas Lake.
THOMAS LAKE. On the 24th of November I was at the King's Arms public-house, in Philip-lane; the prisoner was there; we had dined at one table - the other persons were gone; and he I were alone in the room; I called the waiter to get me some snuff; I took out my box and offered it to her, and I think the prisoner reached out his hand, and handed it across the table to her - she returned with the box and some snuff in it, and gave it to the prisoner to pass on to me, I believe, but I am not quite certain whether he took it or not, but it came into his hands; he took some snuff and, in a short time, went out with the box; I did not see him again till last Saturday; I saw the box at the Mansion House - this is it; I did not know where the prisoner lived, but he knew where I lived.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you give me the snuff-box? A. Yes; I think I did, and you gave it to the waiter, and she returned it; I did not fall asleep in the interim - I got a little drowsy - I was not asleep when you left the room; I saw you go - I made no remark to the waiter about your leaving the room; I waited some time expecting you to return; I never said to the waiter, it would be all right if you had got it; she did not tell me you had it; I went two or three days afterwards to know if you had returned the box, and the answer was you had not called.
Cross-examined by Mr. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did not you put your box into the prisoner's hands? A. Yes; he took it for the purpose of handing it to the waiter, and she went out; I did not afterwards express myself satisfied that it had not been stolen; I had seen him five or six times before, but I did not see him again till Saturday last - I was very angry about it.
JANE CRAGHILL. I live at the King's-arms, Phillip-lane. I saw the prosecutor and the prisoner there; I was called, and the snuff-box was given me to get some snuff; I did not like to go, as it was wet, and I gave it to our young man to go; when I came back, the prisoner took the box, as I supposed, to pass it to Mr. Lake; the prisoner came a day or two afterwards, and I asked him if he took the box, he said he did; I said, it was not a gentlemanly sort of action; he said I might make myself comfortable, as he would return it; he staid at the house about three hours, and had something to drink to the amount of 3s.; but he went away and did not pay - the money belonged to me.
Cross-examined. Q. On your return, did you see the prosecutor? A. Yes; he was not asleep - he told me to give the twopence to the man for fetching the snuff; he was sitting down, but I could not get at him, and I gave the box to the prisoner, but I did not see him put it into his pocket; I ran up as far as Wood-street after the prisoner, and asked him for the money; he said he would not pay.
JOSEPH MARTIN. I received the key of the box of the prisoner, which I gave to Hesketh, who found the duplicate.
ROBERT HESKETH. I went and found the duplicate in the box.
ROBERT ESSEX. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Aldersgate-street. I received this box in pawn on the 24th of November, for 1l.; the ticket is in the name of Joseph Nightingale; I gave the person this duplicate.
Prisoner to JANE CRAGHILL. Q. Did not I return the same day? A. No; it was late before you went away; I did not say the gentleman had been gone more than an hour. GUILTY. Aged 35.
Transported for Seven Years.
